- **On fast-sync machines the criterion is met on the merged daemon**, by the rule, for both rows, with brackets of 0.5 to 1.5%.
- **On slow-sync machines it is not shown.** Half of the rows got no verdict because the model's own rows were 11% apart: the model climbed through both jobs, 3469 to 4227 and 4015 to 4466. The counted rows sit at the line, as M1.T15's row of this daemon did (0.889).
- What separates the two kinds is the work disk: on `sda` (write through, a sync of 3100 MiB/s, which only a host that buffers the writes can give) the daemon's second write costs little; on nvme (write back, 150 MiB/s) it does. Neither is a flash device whose flush is real (M1.T16).
- Nothing was rerun, and no job failed.

#### Without the write log: the in-place store (M1.3r, M1.T18)

The owner asked on 2026-11-12 whether the write log is needed at all, since the product claims the durability of its snapshots and a best-effort recovery point when the daemon dies. The answer has two parts.

**What requires the log is the contract's own rule R10**, that after a power cut the volume is a prefix of the daemon's order with every operation whole. Only a journal gives that. ZFS asks less of a disk: what a completed flush covered is there, and anything written since may be old, new or torn. `skyzfsd serve --store in-place` (M1.3r, skyzfs/skyzfs#43, built as an experiment and merged on 2026-11-13 with the log store still the default) is a store that promises exactly that, named there **the volatile-cache disk**:

- a write goes to its offset in the home image through the page cache and is acknowledged; a flush is an `fdatasync` of that file on the helper thread, and covers what was acknowledged before it was received; a discard does nothing;
- requests run on the pump's thread, with no worker, no readers and no hand-off; there is no log, no index, no checkpoint and nothing to recover at a start;
- a failed write or sync fails the volume for good, and the sync is never tried again;
- what dies with the process is nothing that was acknowledged, because the page cache holds it.

What it does not meet, as its description states first: R10; R11's reopening after a power cut; and after a failed write or sync the blocks no flush covered are left as a power cut leaves them, where the log store leaves them whole. The contract suite reports the six power-cut cases and two failure cases as not met by this store, and a separate test of 200 random programs with simulated power cuts, and as many with failing writes and syncs, checks the weaker promise sector by sector. Owed if it were to become the default: the daemon's failure path on a start that meets a failed disk, the failure marker written on the queue's thread, a mode of the daemon rig that drives it, and no way back to the log store (a flush of the log store does not cover what the in-place store had acknowledged; found by Codex and the review).

**Measured** (M1.T18): four benchmark jobs as M1.T17's, every daemon row built at the experiment's head, the sequence model, log store, in place, model, in place, in place at 1 GiB, model; every row once[^t18runs].

| Job | Disk-sync, work disk | tps in sequence | Log store | In place | In place, second row | In place at 1 GiB |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| A, 24.04 | 3642 MiB/s, `sda1` | 3385, 3281, 3417, 3445, 3395, 3301, 3400 | 0.961 | 1.000 | 0.992 | 0.965 |
| B, 24.04 | 3608 MiB/s, `sda1` | 3231, 2928, 3242, 3375, 3231, 3275, 3432 | 0.887 | 0.982 | 0.949 | 0.962 |
| C, 26.04 | 3596 MiB/s, `sda1` | 3705, 3648, 3765, 3706, 3773, 3636, 3756 | 0.984 | 1.016 | 1.011 | 0.975 |
| D, 26.04 | 193 MiB/s, `nvme0n1p1` | 5712, 5233, 5866, 6189, 5726, 5226, 6083 | 0.879 | 0.986 | 0.933 | 0.852 |

Every bracket was within the rule's 10% (0.0 to 8.0%).

| Machine kind | Store, row | Ratios | Median | Rests on |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Fast-sync | log | 0.961, 0.887, 0.984 | 0.961 | 3 rows, 3 jobs |
| Fast-sync | in place | 1.000, 0.992, 0.982, 0.949, 1.016, 1.011 | **0.996** | 6 rows, 3 jobs |
| Fast-sync | in place at 1 GiB | 0.965, 0.962, 0.975 | **0.965** | 3 rows, 3 jobs |
| Slow-sync | log | 0.879 | | 1 row |
| Slow-sync | in place | 0.986, 0.933 | 0.960 | 2 rows, 1 job |
| Slow-sync | in place at 1 GiB | 0.852 | | 1 row |

- **In place ran at the model's speed**, and beside the log store in the same bracket it was faster in all four jobs: by 4.1%, 10.7%, 3.2% and, on the slow-sync machine, 12.1%. The log row was always the earlier of the two, so their order was not varied.
- **The daemon's CPU fell by more than half:** 9 to 10 s on the pump in a window of 120 s and nothing elsewhere, against 22 to 24 s over the log store's pump, worker and readers. Postgres got 4 to 14 s more CPU in the in-place row than in the log row of the same job.
- **Bounded to 1 GiB on the slow-sync machine it was at 0.852**, one row. The pump used 12 s of CPU there against 9 s unbounded (12 to 15 s over the four jobs' bounded rows). The likely cause is that a read that misses the page cache is served on the pump's thread and holds every other request up while it waits; that is not shown, since a wait uses no CPU and nothing measured the pump's waits. A pool of readers for misses is the remedy if it is the cause, and is not built.
- Three of the four machines were fast-sync, so the slow-sync figures rest on one job.

**The confirming round** (M1.T19): four jobs, the order of the two stores swapped and both bounded as well; the sequence model, in place, log store, model, in place at 1 GiB, log store at 1 GiB, model; every row once; the daemon at the experiment's last head, whose serving path is M1.T18's[^t19runs].

| Job | Disk-sync, work disk | tps in sequence | In place | Log | In place at 1 GiB | Log at 1 GiB |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| J1, 24.04 | 142 MiB/s, `sda1`, write through | 3767, 3783, 3552, 3787, 3448, 3326, 3885 | 1.002 | 0.940 | 0.899 | 0.867 |
| J2, 24.04 | 3548 MiB/s, `sda1`, write through | 3500, 3581, 3432, 3522, 3427, 3355, 3559 | 1.020 | 0.978 | 0.968 | 0.948 |
| J3, 26.04 | 197 MiB/s, `nvme0n1p1`, write back | 5353, 5610, 4587, 5825, 5090, 4968, 6170 | 1.004 | 0.821 | 0.849 | 0.828 |
| J4, 26.04 | 161 MiB/s, `sda1`, write through | 3747, 3677, 3474, 3907, 3485, 3370, 3894 | 0.961 | 0.908 | 0.893 | 0.864 |

Every bracket was within 10% (0.3 to 8.4%). Two of the `sda` machines were slow-sync this time: the device's name does not tell the kind of machine, the measured rate does.

- **In place was ahead of the log store in all eight pairs**, with the order swapped: by 4.3 to 22.3% unbounded and by 2.1 to 3.7% at 1 GiB.
- **Unbounded, in place is at the model's speed on every machine**: 1.002, 1.020, 1.004, 0.961.
- **At 1 GiB on slow-sync machines both stores are under 0.90**: in place 0.899, 0.849, 0.893, the log store 0.867, 0.828, 0.864. On the one fast-sync machine both were over (0.968, 0.948).
- **Where the bound costs:** under the limit the in-place pump's system time rises by 2.9 to 6.0 s per job (from about 8 s to 10.6 to 13.9 s); in the log store the same rise lands on the readers (4.2 to 7.0 s). The daemon's memory group hit its limit about 14,500 times in a bounded window and never in an unbounded one. The daemon's own resident memory is 140 MiB in place and 156 MiB with the log, so what the limit bounds is the page cache of the volume's file. What the pump does in that extra system time has not been profiled.

**The in-place store under the rule, M1.T18's and M1.T19's rows together** (one serving path, two builds):

| Machine kind, row | Ratios | Median | Rests on | By the rule |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Fast-sync, unbounded | 1.000, 0.992, 0.982, 0.949, 1.016, 1.011, 1.020 | 1.000 | 7 rows, 4 jobs | met |
| Fast-sync, 1 GiB | 0.965, 0.962, 0.975, 0.968 | 0.967 | 4 rows, 4 jobs | met |
| Slow-sync, unbounded | 0.986, 0.933, 1.002, 1.004, 0.961 | 0.986 | 5 rows, 4 jobs | met |
| Slow-sync, 1 GiB | 0.852, 0.899, 0.849, 0.893 | **0.873** | 4 rows, 4 jobs | **not met** |

These are rows of the experiment's builds and not of the merged daemon with the in-place store as its only store, which is what the criterion asks for; they say where it will stand. **One cell is open: bounded to 1 GiB on slow-sync machines.** The store reads and writes the volume's file through the page cache, which the limit bounds, while ZFS above it keeps its own cache: the block is cached twice, and under the limit the kernel reclaims on the pump's thread. Whether the store should do direct I/O instead is the open question the design has carried since M1 began (§5.1, "Block cache"); a profile of the bounded row comes first.

**The power-cut replay with the in-place store**, full budget, one run per image[^t18replay]:

| | Ubuntu 24.04 | Ubuntu 26.04 |
|---|---|---|
| Record prefixes checked | 594 of 594 | 596 of 596 |
| fsync marks, sync marks, flush boundaries | 44, 17, 120 | 46, 17, 122 |
| Transaction groups, prefixes deep-checked | 27, 54 | 27, 54 |
| `withheld` | caught | caught |
| `zdb -e -bcc` at the end | 0 | 0 |
| The daemon's starts | 594, of 24 to 58 ms | 596, of 40 to 57 ms |
| Written to the lower disk by the same workload | 181 MiB (430 MiB with the log store) | 181 MiB |
| Failed prefixes | 0 | 0 |

For this store the rig's prefixes follow the kernel's writeback order and not the daemon's, so the run shows the pool surviving states that are not prefixes of the daemon's order. It does not show a disk that loses or reorders unflushed writes in another order than it received them, nor a torn sector; that gap is wider for this store than for the log store, whose own order is a prefix by construction. One run per image.

**Decided on 2026-11-13 (design, decision D5): the in-place store is adopted and the log store is retired.** The design's §4.1, §5.1, §5.2, §5.3 and §5.5 were rewritten for it the same day. The steps: the experiment merges with the log store still the default; M1.3s moves the contract's model in `skyzfs-blk` to the volatile-cache disk and closes what the experiment owed in the daemon; M1.T20 makes the rigs drive the in-place store and name the store they drive; M1.3t makes it the only store and removes the log store, its format, its options and its hooks. Volumes that hold a log are not migrated: there are none outside the rigs. Everything in these notes about the log store, from M1.2a to M1.3p, describes the store that is being removed; it stays as the record of what was built and why it was dropped.

**What the log store's log was for, and what replaces it** (now in the design, §5.2; to be built in M2):

- *A cheap flush.* Not borne out: the in-place flush was as fast or faster on these machines, with less than half the bytes written. Not measured on real flash.
- *The cut as a position in the log, and the cut's blocks kept until uploaded (design §5.2, §5.3).* A cut needs the set of blocks changed since the cut before, frozen at the first flush after the uberblock write, and their content kept against later overwrites until uploaded. The log gave the first for nothing. Without it the daemon needs a map of changed blocks that survives the daemon's death, and a copy made before a block of a pending cut is overwritten. Neither is designed.
- *The ordered stream of M9.* Needs an ordered record of the log device only, which is small.

#### Where the bounded row's loss goes (M1.T22)

The in-place store under a 1 GiB bound on the daemon's memory group stayed under the rule on slow-sync machines (0.873 over four rows, above). M1.T22 added the pump thread's profile, its system calls and the group's reclaim counters to the benchmark and ran the bounded row with them. Six jobs, each one machine for all its rows (the table has the five whose ratios are quoted), the daemon at `zfs-topology`'s head of the day with `--store in-place`, origin delay 0; a traced or recorded row counts for no ratio.

| Job | Machine | Unbounded | 1 GiB | Bracket |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 43357382135 | slow-sync (157 MiB/s) | not run | 0.804, no verdict | 15.9% |
| 43357385356 | fast-sync (3001 MiB/s), a kind not seen before, about 7500 tps | not run | 0.936 | 1.4% |
| 43361592308 | fast-sync (2845 MiB/s) | 1.022 | 0.986 | 1.8% |
| 43361598539 | slow-sync (164 MiB/s) | 0.971 | 0.886 | 7.1% |
| 43367906399 | fast-sync (2780 MiB/s), the same new kind | 1.018 | 0.928 | 0.4% |
| 43373221489 | slow-sync (194 MiB/s) | 0.962 | 0.929 | 9.3% |

**The pump reads the image itself and waits for each read.** On the in-place store only the pump's thread calls `pread64`, about 0.6 times a transaction. Its cost with and without the bound, on the four jobs that traced both (microseconds a call, and the pump's seconds in `pread64` per second):

| Job | Unbounded | 1 GiB |
|---|---|---|
| 43361592308 (fast) | 23 µs, 0.05 s/s | 172 µs, 0.31 s/s |
| 43361598539 (slow) | 6.5 µs, 0.02 s/s | 131 µs, 0.39 s/s |
| 43367906399 (fast) | 4.0 µs, 0.02 s/s | 99 µs, 0.39 s/s |
| 43373221489 (slow) | 6.9 µs, 0.02 s/s | 137 µs, 0.39 s/s |

The first pair, bounded only: 292 µs a call on the slow-sync machine and 92 µs on the fast one. Under the bound the pump's time in `io_uring_enter` falls by about what its time in `pread64` grows. The syncing thread's `fdatasync` took 145 ms a call on the slow-sync machine and 0.39 ms on the fast one.

**Reclaim itself is a small part of the pump's CPU.** Of the pump's kernel samples in the bounded recorded rows (999 Hz, 20 s; 1682 and 5482 samples, 84% and 82% in the kernel): page cache 42% and 44%, wake and switch 14%, page reclaim 6.0% and 6.3%, the memory group 2.6% and 4.1%, write-back from a write 0. All the reclaim is direct, on the daemon's own threads; the group hit its limit ten to twenty-eight thousand times in a 120 s window, and 0.3 to 0.6 file pages a transaction were refaults, pages evicted and read back soon after. Without the bound all of these are 0. The reclaim counts of these runs were read some seconds after the window's end and may be somewhat high; the tooling was corrected afterwards (skyzfs/skyzfs#49).

**Reading.** Under the bound the group evicts pages of the home image that the daemon then reads back from the disk. The slowdown of the reads shows on every machine. The bounded row itself lies about the line: 0.886 and 0.929 on slow-sync machines, 0.986, 0.928 and 0.936 on fast ones, one job each, and one job without a verdict. The last job's reclaim counts were read at the window's end with the corrected tooling: 0.5 refaults a transaction.

**Reads off the pump's thread do not help (M1.T22b).** The first reading was that the pump, waiting for its own reads, carries the loss. An experiment build served the in-place store's reads from four reader threads, buffered as before (branch `skyzfs-coding-reads`, d58a97a), and was measured beside the build it was made from (1280579) in one sequence a job: model, current, experiment, model, experiment, current, model, all bounded at 1 GiB, so that each build ran once in each order. Eight jobs; a job counts when its machine is slow-sync and both brackets are within 10%.

| Job | Machine | Current | Experiment | Experiment over current |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 43370579970 | slow (205 MiB/s), counts | 0.894, 0.890 | 0.889, 0.874 | 0.988 |
| 43370596426 | slow (193 MiB/s), counts | 0.905, 0.896 | 0.902, 0.891 | 0.996 |
| 43370588480 | slow (207 MiB/s), one bracket 12.4% wide | 0.900 (no verdict), 1.025 | 0.892 (no verdict), 0.960 | 0.964 |
| 43362946703 | fast | 0.973, 0.961 | 0.958, 0.954 | 0.989 |
| 43362954573 | fast | 0.983, 0.959 | 0.950, 0.958 | 0.983 |
| 43362962443 | fast | 0.990, 0.963 | 0.900, 0.923 | 0.934 |
| 43376081603 | fast (3644 MiB/s) | 0.978, 0.967 | 0.958, 0.956 | 0.985 |

The eighth job, 43362938498, did not end: see the hang below. Over the two counting jobs the pooled median is 0.895 for the current build and 0.890 for the experiment, four rows each. The experiment is level with the current build on slow-sync machines and one to two percent under it on fast ones, for seven to eight seconds more CPU a window (the reader threads' 7.3 to 7.9 s beside a pump that saves under one) and about twice the CPU pressure in the daemon's group. Refaults are the same in both builds, 0.48 to 0.53 a transaction. So the cost is the eviction and the read from the disk, whichever thread waits for it, and the branch does not become a work package. What follows from that is to keep no cache of the image at all, as the model keeps none: direct I/O on the image, built as an experiment (branch `skyzfs-coding-directio`) and being measured.

**A row that hung (M1.T24).** In job 43362938498 the sixth row, the current build under the 1 GiB bound on a slow-sync machine, never began its window: the daemon said it was serving, and the script's copy of the pool image onto the device (`dd bs=1M oflag=direct conv=sparse`, then `sync`) had not ended 96 minutes later when the job's limit cancelled it. The same build had run the same row on that machine twenty minutes before. Nothing was captured: the copy had no limit and the job's evidence step did not run on a cancel. It happened once in 14 rows of that build under the bound. A reading of the store's code found no wait without a bound outside the kernel (a buffered write under the group's throttling, and `fdatasync`); the author's hypothesis, not shown, is that the kernel killed the daemon for its group's memory, after which the copy waits on a device with no server. A reproduction on the merged daemon did not hang: 90 copies of the image (32768 MiB, about 1015 MiB allocated) with their syncs, each on a new volume and daemon under the bound, 30 a job on one fast-sync and two slow-sync machines (runs 43379878200, 43379882242, 43379886523; a copy took 7 to 14 s and its sync at most 3.8 s). That covers the bare copy on a fresh machine, not a sixth row after five windows. Since M1.T24 every row's copy and sync run under a limit of 600 s, and one that runs out leaves the daemon's status, whether it is alive, its threads' stacks, the group's memory events and the kernel log, and fails the row. The cause is open.

#### Direct I/O on the image (decision D6)

An experiment build (branch `skyzfs-coding-directio`, bec42e9: `serve --store in-place --direct-io --io-threads N`) reads and writes the home image with direct I/O on N threads, one request at the disk a thread; a flush waits for every write in flight. It was measured beside the buffered in-place store it was made from (3af3370). Nine jobs, each one machine for all its rows, one 120 s row a figure, origin delay 0; "1 GiB" is the daemon's memory group. A job counts when its machine is slow-sync and its brackets are within 10%.

| Job | Machine | Bound | Buffered | Direct, 1 thread | Direct, 8 threads |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 43388873406 | slow, counts | 1 GiB | 0.945, 0.942 | 0.971, 0.966 | |
| 43395635762 | slow, counts | 1 GiB | 0.834 | 0.913 | 0.935, 0.936 |
| 43391910068 | slow, counts | none | 0.948 | 0.967 | 0.997 |
| 43391917815 | slow, counts | none | 0.983 | 0.975 | 1.004 |
| 43395486544 | slow, brackets 28.4% and 13.0% | 1 GiB | 0.772, 0.792 | 1.080, 0.851 | |
| 43388888393 | slow, bracket 11.5% | none | 0.938 | 0.947 | |
| 43388882594 | fast | 1 GiB | 0.984, 0.970 | 0.956, 0.952 | |
| 43388892966 | fast | none | 1.019 | 0.957 | |
| 43381605255 (the scout) | fast | 1 GiB | 0.972 | 0.958 | 0.950 (32 threads: 0.943; 8 threads on a zero-filled image: 0.963) |

- On slow-sync machines eight threads are above one thread in the three jobs that ran both (by 0.022 under the bound, 0.030 and 0.029 without it) and above the buffered store in all three (by 0.101, 0.049 and 0.021). One thread is above the buffered store in four of five comparisons. Eight threads under the bound rest on one job.
- On fast-sync machines one thread is under the buffered store in all four jobs, by 0.014 to 0.062, and at 0.94 or above. Eight threads there rest on the scout's one row.
- Every direct row has no reclaim in the daemon's group: 0 refaults and nothing scanned, against 489 to 509 refaults and about 1950 MiB scanned per thousand transactions for the buffered store under the bound.
- The daemon's CPU, one machine (43395635762), user and system seconds a window: buffered, pump 1.6 and 9.6; one thread, pump 2.7 and 7.2 and the I/O thread 0.6 and 6.4; eight threads, pump 2.4 and 8.0 and the I/O threads 0.9 and 8.0. Four to six seconds more system time. Postgres waited on I/O 20.8% of the window with the buffered store, 30.3% with one thread and 23.0% with eight: reads that the page cache served now come off the disk.
- Filling the image with zeros at create took 83 s for the benchmark's image and moved nothing beyond one run's noise. No row hung.
- On the simulated disk the power-cut test with garbage, 200 seeds: at 16 KiB pages 120 lost blocks buffered and 0 direct, at 64 KiB 113 and 0, with one I/O thread and with four; with the simulated direct write widened to the page the test fails at seed 4.

Decided on these figures: direct I/O is the in-place store's I/O, eight threads by default (M1.3y). The rule is shown again on that daemon.

#### A direct write into a hole, at a power cut (M1.T27)

Design §4.2 says that over a hole or an unwritten extent a direct write that has returned still needs the file system's commit to be readable after a power cut. M1.T27 measured it in the power-cut replay, the cut on the disk under the daemon's files and each prefix read through a daemon started as after a reboot. The course: 16 direct writes of 4 KiB to the daemon's device on a 256 MiB volume, a flush after every third, the last two unflushed. Six courses a job: ext4 and XFS, each on a sparse image, on one preallocated with `fallocate` (unwritten extents) and on one written through with `create --zero-fill`; `filefrag` confirms the three kinds. The daemon is the direct I/O package's build (b4a4068). Full budget, every prefix, run 43429346339, one run an image; a sampled run before it (43428255511) agrees.

Per course: prefixes checked, prefixes at which a returned write read as zeros, and of those the prefixes at which the write's data was already on the disk.

| Image | File system | Sparse | `fallocate` | Written through |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| `ubuntu-24.04` | ext4 | 108 / 73 / 56 | 100 / 65 / 48 | 73 / 27 / 0 |
| `ubuntu-24.04` | XFS | 72 / 39 / 22 | 73 / 39 / 22 | 66 / 27 / 0 |
| `ubuntu-26.04` | ext4 | 110 / 74 / 57 | 102 / 66 / 49 | 74 / 27 / 0 |
| `ubuntu-26.04` | XFS | 72 / 39 / 22 | 73 / 39 / 22 | 66 / 27 / 0 |
| `ubuntu-26.04-arm` | ext4 | 110 / 74 / 57 | 102 / 66 / 49 | 88 / 41 / 0 |
| `ubuntu-26.04-arm` | XFS | 72 / 39 / 22 | 73 / 39 / 22 | 66 / 27 / 0 |

- No violation in any of the 18 courses: at every prefix at or after a flush that completed after a write, the block holds what was written, and before it the block is zeros or the write, whole. The control, a flush acknowledged with its data withheld, is caught in all 18.
- The third figure is the effect in question: the data is on the disk and the file does not show it, because the extent change is not committed. It is there on a sparse and on a preallocated image, on both file systems and both architectures, and never on an image written through. `fallocate` is no better than a hole on XFS and a little better on ext4.
- The second figure also counts writes lost from the disk's volatile cache, which every kind of image loses alike (the recording logs a completed write at the lower disk's next flush).

All of it is loss that design §4.1 allows. Decided on these figures: the image stays sparse (design §10).

The table rests on one run an image, and a course's length varies from run to run: ext4 on a preallocated image had 100 prefixes on `ubuntu-24.04` in the table's run and 119 in a later one (43498059751). The rig is merged (skyzfs/skyzfs#63) and runs on every pull request that runs the daemon's replay jobs. It judges three things: every checked prefix as §4.1 allows, the control caught, and on a written-through image no prefix with the data on the disk and zeros in the file (0 in all four such courses of run 43515269154, on the merged direct I/O daemon).

#### Direct I/O as built (M1.3y)

`skyzfsd serve` (then with `--store in-place`, an option removed in M1.3t-d) reads and writes the home image with `O_DIRECT` on a pool of I/O threads, eight by default (`--io-threads`, 1 to 64); there is no buffered served mode. What the package added to the experiment:

- The start asks `statx` for the direct-I/O units and refuses, by reason and without failing the volume, a file system that reports none, or whose offset unit does not divide 4 KiB.
- The store orders overlapping requests itself: a read waits for an overlapping write on the threads, a write or write-zeroes for an overlapping read or write, a flush for every write. Seven planted defects each fail a test and none hangs.
- A thread that panics or dies fails the volume like a failed write.
- The status file is version 9: the threads, requests on them and waiting now, how many waited, and the highs. `zeroes_applied` has a value 4, "written as zeros by direct I/O".
- The start's sync and cache drop stay, and stay after the log store goes: they cover a file system that takes direct I/O and serves it through its cache.
- `serve --request-log PATH`, in the build with test hooks only, records each request the kernel hands the daemon and each answer. It cannot show a commit the driver refused: the binding drops command results other than OK (M1.3aa).

Not shown: ext4 with `data=journal` is read as reporting no direct-I/O unit, so that the start refuses it; no rig has run it. The throughput rule and the thread default on the merged daemon are still to be measured.

#### The thread count on the merged daemon (M1.T29, first round)

Twelve jobs on 2026-11-13 on the merged direct I/O daemon (zfs-topology a234314; the arm64 jobs from a branch that adds only runner options), each job one machine for all its rows, each row once. Ten rows a job: model, t8, t1, model, t8-1g, t1-1g, model, t4, t4-1g, model, with t1 before t8 in half the jobs. tpcb, 8 clients, 120 s, no origin delay, postgres in 1 GiB; "1g" is the daemon in a 1 GiB memory cgroup; tN is `--io-threads N`. A row counts only when its two model rows differ by 10% or less.

Median ratio to the model, with the counted rows:

| Machines | Bound | 1 thread | 4 threads | 8 threads |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| amd64, fast syncs (3 jobs) | none | 0.957 (0.938, 0.957, 0.958) | 0.947 (0.944, 0.947, 0.955) | 0.952 (0.947, 0.952, 0.955) |
| amd64, fast syncs | 1 GiB | 0.947 (0.938, 0.947, 0.948) | 0.959 (0.943, 0.959, 0.962) | 0.954 (0.945, 0.954, 0.957) |
| amd64, slow syncs (5 jobs) | none | 0.924 (0.914, 0.924, 0.991) | 1.012 (0.965, 1.012, 1.053) | 0.963 (0.826, 0.963, 0.993) |
| amd64, slow syncs | 1 GiB | 0.970 (0.938, 0.970, 0.982) | 0.972 (0.869, 0.972, 0.997) | 0.877 (0.699, 0.877, 1.010) |
| arm64 (4 jobs) | none | 0.962 (0.896, 0.958, 0.965, 0.974) | 0.946 (0.878, 0.918, 0.973, 0.980) | 0.978 (0.894, 0.971, 0.985, 0.986) |
| arm64 | 1 GiB | 0.964 (0.947, 0.961, 0.966, 0.981) | 0.935 (0.930, 0.932, 0.937, 0.997) | 0.968 (0.947, 0.951, 0.986, 0.987) |

Eight threads minus one, in the same bracket: fast-sync -0.003, -0.005, +0.009 unbounded and +0.009, +0.007, +0.007 bounded; arm64 -0.071, +0.012, +0.075, +0.027 and +0.021, 0.000, -0.010, +0.005; slow-sync +0.002, +0.049, -0.098 and +0.028, -0.061, -0.271.

- On fast-sync amd64 the three thread counts are within 1.2 points of each other in no order, and on arm64 within 3.3. Every such cell's median is 0.935 or above.
- The slow-sync cells do not decide between one thread and eight. Six of the twelve brackets on the nvme machines were over 10% (two rows of each kind there have no verdict), and single rows ran from 0.699 to 1.286. The lowest row, 0.699 (eight threads, bounded, run 43498281407), fell on a stall of the machine's disk: 61 ms a flush against 0 to 1 ms in the rows beside it. One row cannot show that the stall and not the thread count made it.
- So the ground of D6's default (eight threads above one on slow-sync machines, by 0.022 to 0.030 in three jobs of the experiment's build) is neither reproduced nor contradicted. The default stays eight, and the bounded slow-sync cell, where eight threads' median is 0.877 over three rows, gets a round of its own.
- One of the slow-sync machines had the disk the notes knew as fast (`sda1`, write through) at 165 MiB/s; the disk's name no longer tells the kind, and a machine is classed by its measured rate. Its brackets were the steadiest of the round (0.1% to 0.9%). The arm64 images' disks are write through at 249 to 345 MiB/s.

Runs: amd64 43497647957, 43498216158, 43498229135 (fast), 43498241766, 43498255284, 43498268163, 43498281407, 43498292940 (slow); arm64 43498318359, 43498330574, 43498341995, 43498353804.

**Second round, the bounded cell.** Eight more `ubuntu-24.04` jobs, the same daemon, every daemon row bounded to 1 GiB: model, t8, t1, model, t1, t8, model, t8, t1, model, with t1 first in every second job, so each job gives three pairs of eight threads against one in the same bracket. Five machines had the slow-sync nvme disk (147 to 202 MiB/s), two the fast `sda1` (3104, 3268), and one a kind not seen before: `dm-0`, write through, 2698 MiB/s.

| Machines | Brackets counted | 1 thread | 8 threads | 8 minus 1, same bracket |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| nvme, slow syncs (5 jobs) | 10 of 15 | 0.987 (0.930 to 1.235) | 1.013 (0.957 to 1.437) | +0.018 (-0.021, -0.015, +0.007, +0.012, +0.013, +0.023, +0.035, +0.039, +0.111, +0.202) |
| `sda1`, fast syncs (2 jobs) | 6 of 6 | 0.951 | 0.956 | +0.007 (-0.006, -0.002, +0.006, +0.008, +0.008, +0.012) |
| `dm-0` (1 job) | 3 of 3 | 0.901 (0.896, 0.901, 0.911) | 0.909 (0.902, 0.909, 0.913) | +0.001 (-0.002, +0.001, +0.017) |

- The first round's 0.877 for eight threads under the bound on slow-sync machines is not reproduced: over ten rows the median is 1.013, and eight of the ten pairs favour eight threads. On fast-sync machines the two are within a point, as before. The default stays eight, settled for M1; the gain is small.
- One nvme job (43513180484) had three of its four model rows slowed by its disk (1.34 to 1.69 ms a write against 0.14 to 0.78 in the other nvme jobs' model rows; 2587 to 3378 tps against 3549), and both its counted brackets hold such a row, so its ratios (1.235, 1.437, 0.958, 1.069) rest on slowed models. Without it: one thread 0.987, eight 0.997, the difference +0.0125 over eight pairs, six positive.
- The `dm-0` machine put both thread counts at 0.90 to 0.91, about four points under `sda1`, with the model at 7338 to 7422 tps and the daemon at 6639 to 6759. One machine; it bears on the rule's median, not on the thread count.
- A rule for a stalled row was fixed before this round: the work disk's time per flush is 10 ms or more. It marked no row here, and it sees almost nothing: the runners' work disks are mounted without barriers and get one flush in a 120 s row on nvme and none on a write-through disk, so the first round's "61 ms a flush" was one flush. A second rule was drawn from this round's figures and holds from the next round on: a row is stalled when its work disk's time per write is more than twice the median of its topology on its class of machine (medians fixed from this round: model 0.53 ms on nvme, 0.165 on `sda1`, 0.13 on `dm-0`; daemon 0.165, 0.10, 0.09). On this round it would mark the three slowed model rows above and one daemon row whose bracket was already too wide. Since it was drawn after the figures were seen, nothing here rests on it.

Runs: 43513120960, 43513132569, 43513144817, 43513168011, 43513180484 (nvme), 43513192085, 43513203524 (`sda1`), 43513156369 (`dm-0`).

#### What the start refuses, and a sync that fails (M1.T30)

Pins added to the daemon rig on 2026-11-14 (skyzfs/skyzfs#72):

- **A failed command is pinned at zero** at the end of every check (`commands_failed` in the status, M1.3aa).
- **ext4 with `data=journal` is refused at the start**: exit 1, "the file system offers no direct I/O on it", nothing served, the volume not failed, and the same volume served again on `data=ordered`. 10 of 10 runs on `ubuntu-24.04` (16e66e9), then every pull-request run on `ubuntu-24.04`, `ubuntu-26.04` and `ubuntu-26.04-arm`. The home image is byte-identical after the refused start and no failure marker appears. Until M1.3t the start still checkpoints the log store on its way into the in-place store, so `log.super` and the log's segment are rewritten; the pin names that one allowance and prints what changed.
- **Pages of the 8 GiB image in the cache** after 16 MiB written to the device by direct I/O and synced: the current daemon, 4 pages in every serve (15 runs); the daemon from before direct I/O (cd0e763), 4096 pages (the whole 16 MiB) on `data=ordered`, on `data=journal` and with `chattr +j`, in two runs. A file marked `chattr +j` on `data=ordered` is served by the current daemon with 4 pages cached: as testing reads ext4, the per-file flag does not take effect under delayed allocation, ext4's default. A first count of the whole image for the old daemon on `data=journal` was the rig's own read before the serve, found by Codex, and is void.
- **Check g's first course had a premise that held by luck**: that a write or flush reaches the daemon after the disk under it fails. In 75 of 76 jobs before the fix it did. On `ubuntu-24.04` after about 22:30 UTC on 2026-11-13 it stopped holding: ZFS suspended the pool on its own failed reads (49 to 249) before any write reached the device, the daemon had nothing to fail on, and the check failed on three pull requests that do not touch the serving path. The runner image and kernels were the same before and after; why it became frequent is not known. The rig now writes to the device itself, by `O_DIRECT|O_SYNC`, right after the switch, and judges by the daemon's counters whether a write or flush reached it; if none did the line is the rig's error. With the fix the premise held in 10 of 10 runs on `ubuntu-24.04` and in every pull-request run since.
- The negative control (cd0e763, run 43541748747) differs in exactly the five lines the workflow names.

#### The log store removed (M1.3t)

On 2026-11-14 (skyzfs/skyzfs#69). The in-place store is the only store. Until M1.3t-d `--store in-place` was accepted and changed nothing and `--store log` was refused with a message naming M1.3t; since M1.3t-d (skyzfs/skyzfs#79) `serve` has no `--store`, and any form of it is an unknown option. What changed:

- **The volume file.** `volume`, 60 bytes: magic `SKYZVOL1`, the volume's id, size, block size, the write unit (the larger of the system's page and the file system's block at create), a random identity, and a CRC-32C. Written once at create, as `volume.new`, synced, renamed, the directory synced; a create cut short leaves none. A start refuses, by reason and with the volume not failed: no volume file, a damaged one, a version it does not know, or a log store's `log.super` ("create the volume again"). `log.failed` became `volume.failed`.
- **Failure codes** 1, 2, 3 and 5 are no longer given; the note's decoder still reads them. The pump's acknowledged-position plumbing, compiled and idle after M1.3t, was removed in M1.3t-b (skyzfs/skyzfs#74).
- **A power-cut test of the start's own sync**, now the only one: writes acknowledged by an instance that is killed, a new start, a power cut before any flush; the writes survive. With the sync removed it fails.
- **The status file** stayed at version 10 until M2.1 (skyzfs/skyzfs#92), whose version 11 drops the log's fields and the store's time fields and adds the count of cuts; until then the log's fields read zero, and so do the store's time fields, since the in-place store keeps no time account (the benchmark's `skz_time.py` lines for the store are empty). Whether it keeps one is decided when a measurement needs it.
- **The owner's requirement on the tests** (only tests of the log's internals go): 216 tests left `skyzfs-local` and 44 came, 22 of them ports of properties that had used the log store as their target. The pull request's description lists every deleted test, whether it tested the log's internals or a property of the contract, pump or lifecycle, and which in-place test holds that property now. `skyzfs-blk`'s suite, model and planted faults are unchanged (144 tests). The workspace went from 972 tests to 799. The review found three properties held more weakly than before (a waker called once per completion held nowhere; two ports whose planted breach passed every test) and they were restored, each shown by its plant. The log store's five tests of its time account have no in-place counterpart.
- **What the rigs judge:** on a pull request nothing changed, since they already drove the in-place store and every line about a log read NA. A start refused on ext4 with `data=journal` now changes no file of the volume (the rig printed "new or rewritten: none" on all three images).
- **Found on the way:** a start right after a start could find its own read-ahead still in the kernel's cache and refuse; the start's look at the image now reads alone, without read-ahead. Codex found two more: `create --zero-fill` wrote its zeros after the volume file, and a wrapper did not forward the new trait method.

Not done: the home image's file system is not tested against the write unit in the simulated disk (at the base neither), M1.3ae.

#### The M1 exit round (2026-11-14)

Planned before it ran and dispatched by the testing session on build 848ab28 (`zfs-topology` with #73 to #79 merged; `skyzfs-ci` green, run 43571802602), the daemon as it ships: the in-place store, direct I/O, eight threads, no serve arguments. Every run ended green.

**The benchmark.** Thirteen jobs, each on its own machine (thirteen boot ids): ten `ubuntu-24.04`, alternating which daemon row comes first, and three `ubuntu-24.04-arm`, the image of M1.T29's arm64 rows. Each job ran ten rows on one machine, model (m), unbounded daemon (U) and daemon bounded by `TB_SKYZFSD_MEM=1G` (B): m, U, B, m, B, U, m, U, B, m (B and U swapped in the B-first jobs). tpcb, eight clients, 120 s, postgres at 1 GiB, `origin_delay_ms` 0, every daemon row pinned to 848ab28 (`TB_SKYZFSD_REF`, `TB_SKZ_TIME_REF`). A ratio is the row's tps over the mean of its two enclosing model rows; the testing session's figures match each job's own table to 0.001. A row counts when its bracket is within 10% and no row of the bracket stalled: the flush rule (a flush of 10 ms or more; no row of the round reached it, the highest work-disk flush was 1.00 ms) and, as a second reading, the write rule of M1.T29 (a write time over twice its class's median).

- **What each rule left out.** Over 10% wide: six rows, four of job 7 (brackets of 16.7% and 37.1%) and two of job 12 (12.3%). The flush rule: nothing. The write rule: every row of job 7, whose daemon rows wrote at 0.35 to 0.70 ms against its class's 0.165 and whose last model row at 1.50 ms against 0.53; and every row of job 10, model rows included, a slow-sync sda machine (the second seen, after M1.T29's at 165 MiB/s) judged against medians drawn from fast-sync sda machines, though its brackets were 4.7 to 5.9% wide and its ratios 0.970 to 1.004. The sda cells under the flush rule alone (nine rows, three jobs) are the fuller figure. arm64 has no write medians, so both readings are the same there.
- **Job 7.** Its machine moved by about a third while it ran (model rows 2423, 2864, 3013, 2071). Under the flush rule alone two of its rows count, B 0.842 and U 0.588 from a 5.1% bracket, the two lowest rows of the nvme cells, which still hold medians of 0.992 and 1.011. U and B ran the same daemon in the same way: in all 78 daemon rows the bounded group held 0 to 1 MiB of file pages, never reached its limit, and was 156 to 157 MiB resident, so the bound bound nothing (direct I/O keeps the image out of the page cache). On the other amd64 machines U and B of one bracket differ by at most 0.044, median 0.007; on arm64 by up to 0.085, median 0.029, neither row consistently lower. Job 7 is read as its machine.
- **Below 0.90, counted:** one row, job 12's B 0.877 (bracket 3.1%), in a cell whose median is 0.938.
- **dm-0.** One of ten amd64 jobs drew such a machine; three rows a cell, all at 0.916 to 0.932, short of the four rows from two jobs. M1.T29 saw the same class once, at 0.909 bounded.
- **By the kinds of machine the rule was fixed with** ("The rule the criterion is judged by", above: slow-sync and fast-sync, and arm64 since M1.T21). The table above classes by work disk, as the lead planned the round in its message to the testing session; that plan was not committed, and the review session pointed out (skyzfs/skyzfs#2, comment 6875486340) that the rule as committed classes by sync rate. By it every cell is met and none is short: slow-sync amd64 (jobs 1, 3, 6 to 10, buffered sync 178 to 207 MiB/s) 0.994 unbounded and 1.006 bounded over nineteen rows from seven jobs (1.001 and 1.012 over fifteen rows from five jobs with the write rule); fast-sync amd64 (jobs 2, 4, 5, 2759 to 3226 MiB/s, dm-0 among them) 0.956 and 0.957 over nine rows from three jobs; arm64 as in the table. The verdict rests on this reading; the work-disk table is the finer view.

**The power-cut replay** (run 43573410351, `server: daemon`, `daemon_budget: full`, `daemon_ref` 848ab28), one run per image. `ubuntu-26.04`: every record prefix, 2690 of 2690; 130 of 130 flush boundaries; 30 transaction groups deep-checked at 60 prefixes; `zdb -bcc` exit 0; controls as expected (withheld caught by the `fsync` check, stuck by a device read); exit 0 in 4767 s. `ubuntu-26.04-arm`: 2619 of 2619; 128 of 128; 29 groups, 58 deep checks; `zdb` exit 0; controls as expected; exit 0 in 5444 s. Both summaries: "Design §9's replay criterion: shown by this run." The writes into a hole on the same run: every checked prefix as §4.1 allows on both images, six courses each, every `withheld` control caught.

**The replay on the 24.04 images** (run 43582992305, the same inputs, at the review's suggestion; `zfsutils-linux` 2.2.2-0ubuntu9.5, older than 26.04's). `ubuntu-24.04`: every record prefix, 2642 of 2642; 128 of 128 flush boundaries; marks `fsync` 54 of 54 and sync 17 of 17; 29 transaction groups deep-checked at 58 prefixes, none failed; `zdb -e -bcc` exit 0; controls as expected (withheld caught by the `fsync` check at prefix 2008, stuck by a device read); exit 0; main pass 5805 s, the job 99.6 of its 120 minutes. `ubuntu-24.04-arm`: 2590 of 2590; 123 of 123; `fsync` 52 of 52, sync 17 of 17; 29 groups, 58 deep checks; `zdb` exit 0; controls as expected (withheld at prefix 2005); exit 0; main pass 6158 s, 105.2 of 120 minutes. The writes into a hole passed on both. A full run on the 24.04 images comes within 15 to 20 minutes of the job's limit, against 34 and 39 minutes a job on 26.04; a slower runner could cut it before its summary. (The summaries' sampled flush-boundary row reads one fewer than the granularity line, 127 and 122; the review read it as an overlap in the counting.)

**The daemon rig** (run 43573413024, `daemon_ref` 848ab28), one run per image, judged again by the lead with 848ab28's judge, exit 0 on each: Ubuntu 24.04 (7.0.0-1014-aws), 26.04 (7.0.0-1019-azure), 24.04 on arm64 (7.0.0-1017-azure) and 26.04 on arm64 (7.0.0-1019-azure). Each: 47 PASS, 5 FAIL (the controls), 2 NA (the reference server's count of writes applied and not acknowledged, `NOT_ON_THE_DAEMON`), 1 WAITING (`pin_failed_volume_while_a_request_is_held`); the suite's cases 13 WEAKER, 12 NOT_EXERCISED, 1 PASS; no `zfs_stuck` mark. `pin_kill_under_load` passed with writes in flight at 20 of 20 kills, and `pin_device_waits_for_a_server` with the device QUIESCED at 30 of 30 looks in 300 s.

**Verdict (the lead).** Every M1 exit criterion of design §9 is shown on 848ab28: the kill test on all four images, the full replay on all four images, and the throughput rule in every cell of slow-sync amd64, fast-sync amd64 and arm64, unbounded and bounded. In the finer view by work disk the dm-0 kind has too few rows. M1 exited on 2026-11-14; gate G1 is answered yes. The review session checked every figure against its run (all thirteen benchmark summaries, the four daemon rig artifacts and both replay summaries) and found them as written. The replay on the two 24.04 images, whose `zdb` is older (2.2.2), was run after and passed (above).

#### The power-cut replay through the direct I/O daemon

Full budget, run 43497336155, zfs-topology a234314, one run an image. `ubuntu-26.04`: every record prefix, 2587 of 2587; 133 of 133 flush boundaries; 28 transaction groups, deep-checked at 56 prefixes; `zdb -bcc` exit 0; both controls caught; 82 minutes. `ubuntu-26.04-arm`: 2636 of 2636; 135 of 135; 29 groups, 58 prefixes; exit 0; both controls caught; 96 minutes. Design §9's replay criterion is shown for this daemon on both architectures. It is run again after the log store's removal (M1.3t).

#### A call that returned after the kill (M1.T28)

The first daemon rig run on the direct I/O daemon failed check c on `ubuntu-26.04`: in the conformance suite through the block device, a read found one write's blocks where a second, overlapping write should have won (run 43430825700, volume 60 of seed 0x5ca1ab1e). `skyzfs-blkcheck --replay` reproduced it from the tool's trace, which showed only that the rig's bookkeeping agreed with itself.

What it was, from a recurrence with the daemon's request log and the kernel's block trace on one clock (run 43455086383, pass 3): the kernel issued the second write whole; the old daemon applied and answered it; the kernel completed it, about 2 ms before the kill. The tool's `pwrite` returned only after the next daemon had taken over. A direct write to a block device returns after the page cache of its range is invalidated, and a buffered read by udev, probing the device after the suite's discard, held a page of that range and was itself waiting at the killed daemon. The tool counted the write as in flight at the death, and the model took a call in flight at a death as issued again, after whatever was acknowledged meanwhile. The daemon and the kernel did what the contract asks; what the disk held is one of the orders two concurrent writes may have. ZFS does not take this path: the wait is in the block device's `O_DIRECT` path for a userspace caller.

Counts, check c on the daemon rig since M1.3s, each job a first pass and a pass with the delay:

| | `ubuntu-24.04` | `ubuntu-26.04` | `ubuntu-26.04-arm` |
|---|---|---|---|
| Buffered daemon | 10 jobs, 0 failed | 10 jobs and 10 traced passes, 0 failed | 9 jobs, 0 failed |
| Direct I/O daemon, before the fix | 3 jobs, 0 failed | 3 jobs, 2 failed (43430825700, 43453345076); 40 traced first passes, 1 failed | 3 jobs, 0 failed |

Every failure was the same volume of the same seed. Why only `ubuntu-26.04` with direct I/O is not shown: its udev watches `ublkb*` (640 change events in a probe's discard mode against 1 in the others), and `ubuntu-24.04` issued no udev reads at all during check c.

What was ruled out on the way: the kernel splitting the call, or a fallback to buffered writes. 0 of 13,500 overlapping direct writes were split or fell back (runs 43443374413 and 43447103915), alone, after a discard, and beside a buffered reader.

The fix has two halves.

- **The rig** (skyzfs/skyzfs#59): check c runs with udev's watch on `ublkb*` off, and keeps the request log and the block trace for a pass where a case failed. With the watch off and the model unchanged, 10 of 10 jobs on `ubuntu-26.04` with the direct I/O daemon passed (20 passes; runs 43463180252 to 43463225632). That removes this trigger only.
- **The model** (skyzfs/skyzfs#60): a request in flight at a daemon loss that the lost daemon applied is either issued again or was completed and is not. Only a harness that says a completion can be held past the loss gets this reading, which is the device harness and `--replay`; the in-memory and the stores' suites keep the strict one, pinned by a planted fault. A request is still never lost or reordered against an acknowledged one. What the model no longer catches through a device: a server that acknowledges a reissued write without applying it, when the old daemon had applied it. From a block device's client the two cannot be told apart; the request log can.

What shows the model's half: five volumes whose failures a kernel made (three from the runs above, two provoked) fail under the old model and are clean under the new. The repeat rig can provoke the case (skyzfs/skyzfs#62: the watch on, each device opened for writing and closed every 20 ms, and a reader of each device's last 256 KiB with the cache dropped after each read). Provoked, the old model failed 2 of 20 passes (43476467345, 43479660648) and the new 0 of 20 (43479646883, 43479653721), one runner a run; at that rate 0 of 20 happens by chance about one time in eight, so the runs alone do not show it. Two earlier ways of provoking, without the reader at the device's end, gave 0 of 10 each.

On the first rig run of the merged direct I/O daemon with both halves (run 43492027748), all three images passed, the request log was kept for 61 volumes a pass, and 6 to 11 calls an image had been completed by the old server and were issued again by the tool.

A lesson for the rig is in `CLAUDE.md`: a replay that agrees with the rig is not a verdict on the server.

#### With barriers on, the runners' flush costs nothing (M1.T16)

One job per image on 2026-11-12, each on one machine, each row once[^t16runs]. The job remounted the work disk with barriers before its first row, and `nobarrier` was gone from the first row to the last. The daemon is the merged one with M1.3p and before M1.3q. "ref" is the ublk project's reference server.

Ubuntu 24.04 (work disk `nvme0n1p1`, disk-sync 200 MiB/s, the model rows 2.1% apart):

| Row | tps | WAL fsync, µs | Work disk's flushes per transaction | ms per flush | Ratio |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| model | 4478 | 519 | 0.38 | 0.09 | |
| daemon | 4144 | 587 | 0.37 | 0.10 | 0.916 |
| daemon at 1 GiB | 4062 | 569 | 0.38 | 0.09 | 0.897 |
| model | 4575 | 511 | 0.38 | 0.10 | |
| ref | 4718 | 489 | 0.38 | 0.09 | |
| model | 4872 | 474 | 0.38 | 0.10 | |

Ubuntu 26.04 (work disk `sda1`, disk-sync 176 MiB/s, the model rows 3.1% apart), where the work disk's flushes stayed at 0.00 per transaction in every row:

| Row | tps | WAL fsync, µs | Ratio |
|---|---|---|---|
| model | 3339 | 677 | |
| daemon | 3219 | 713 | 0.949 |
| daemon at 1 GiB | 2920 | 760 | 0.861 |
| model | 3445 | 656 | |
| ref | 3613 | 605 | |
| model | 3653 | 615 | |

- **On the nvme machine a flush reaches the device and costs about 0.1 ms.** There is about one per commit of the intent log, the same for the model and the daemon. A device that made data durable on flash would take longer, so this virtual disk most likely acknowledges a flush without doing that. Whether anything got slower by it cannot be read from these rows: the rows without barriers are from other machines.
- **On the `sda` machine no flush reaches the device even with barriers.** The split is by disk and not by image. The review counted eight of the day's runs: every machine with `sda` had counted no flush since its boot, on both images, and the one whose `write_cache` was read says `write through`, so the block layer completes a flush itself and `nobarrier` changes nothing there; every nvme machine had counted some, and the one read says `write back`. Five of the day's twelve machines had `sda`. This job did not read the field itself (M1.T15 adds that).
- **So the hosted runners cannot say what a flush costs on real flash**, with barriers or without. That measurement needs a machine with a local flash device (design §10). A comparison with and without barriers on one runner was not ordered: it would price a flush that costs nothing.
- The ratios, 0.916 and 0.949 unbounded and 0.897 and 0.861 at 1 GiB, are in the range of the rows before them. They are not added to the criterion's medians, because the mount differs from those rows'.

#### Open at this writing (2026-11-14, after M1's exit)

- What a device flush costs the daemon and the model on real flash: not measurable on the hosted runners (M1.T16, above); it needs a machine with a local flash device.
- The dm-0 kind of work disk, in the finer view: one job in the exit round, three rows a cell at 0.916 to 0.932; M1.T29 saw it once at 0.909.
- Pages larger than 4 KiB on arm64: the hosted images have 4 KiB pages only.
- Stuck ZFS after a suspension on arm64, 2 in 44 check-d jobs, cause unknown; the rig now names it and stops (M1.T32).
- A benchmark row through the in-place daemon under the bound hung once, cause unknown (M1.T24).
- A refused ublk commit stops the daemon for a take-over (M1.3ac part 3, exit status 8; the kernel's side shown in M1.T33); what a refused fetch does to a start is to be seen first (M1.T36, then M1.3ah).
- The status file still carries the log store's fields (`unstored`, the store times), read 0; they go with its next version.
- The image stays sparse (M1.T27); a full file system still fails the volume, until the store accounts for local space (M3).
- Whether the criterion should be judged on runners with barriers on is the user's decision.

### Risks in detail (design §10)

The design's risk table carries these four rows in short. In full:

| Risk | Consequence | Mitigation |
|---|---|---|
| A call into the local store hangs and neither returns nor fails, as a write or a sync on a sick disk can | The requests behind it are held without bound, the volume is never declared failed, and an orderly stop waits with them. At a failure the daemon's delete of its device waits for the held requests, and with it every add and delete of a ublk device on the node (§4.2) | Since M1.3d a failed volume answers what it holds before it deletes, which removes the wait at a failure in every case but one: the queue's thread stuck in a write of the acknowledged position that began before the failure (§4.2). Open, and not decided with M1.3d: whether a store call that exceeds a bound should itself fail the volume, and what an orderly stop does when its flush does not return. The two places for a bound and their costs: the pump's stop leaves what the store holds to the next instance after a stated time, which costs no data but needs a process that can end with a worker inside a system call; or the store declares the volume failed when a call has not returned in a stated time, which fails a volume for a disk that was only slow. Neither is built; decided when a disk that hangs has been produced on the rig. |
| A pool that only writes does not notice that its device is gone: ZFS retries failed writes without end and suspends only at a failed read (§4.2, measured on the daemon, four runs per image) | The pool stays ONLINE for minutes over a deleted device, spends a core on retries and tens of thousands of events a second, and with its log device goes on acknowledging synchronous writes: 192 MB in one run. Applications are told "written" for data that no transaction group can carry. A node lost in that state loses all of it, beyond the bound D2 states | The daemon says it in every message of a failure or a refusal and in `skyzfsd status` (M1.3d). The agent makes the pool read, which suspends it within a second or two, and reports it lost for this boot (M2); until then that is the operator's step. Open: the surest command, and what export, reopen, clear and zed do in that state (M1.T8). |
| The daemon does not reach the model's throughput on machines whose disk takes its time over a sync: 0.82 to 0.91 of it there with M1.3j built (§9) | M1 does not exit on its throughput criterion. Writes to the device wait about three times as long on such a machine as on one with a fast sync | Reads left the worker (M1.3j) and are no longer the cause. What is left is writes waiting behind the worker's syncs, whose time is the page cache's backlog at the disk's rate (§9). Writeback started early (M1.3k) takes the log's syncs away and not the home image's, which a step writes just before it syncs it (§9, one job). The home image's sync went to a helper thread (M1.3m): the waits fall and the throughput does not move, because what is left is the daemon's CPU on a small machine and, with its cache bounded, reads queueing on one reader (§9). Several readers and a cheaper status thread are built (M1.3n) and the criterion is judged by bracketed ratios under a rule fixed in advance: on slow-sync machines the merged daemon's median is 0.871 unbounded and it is lower bounded, against 0.90. Postgres waits beside the daemon and does not work harder; a third of the wait is a dearer sync of its write-ahead log on a disk it shares with the daemon's files, the rest is not placed (§9). Fast-sync machines, which are what node-local flash is like, have been at 0.92 to 0.98; whether the criterion should be judged there is the user's decision and is open. |
| Licensing | The frontend lives in CDDL code. The daemon is FSL. | Publish the frontend under CDDL and the protocol header under a permissive licence, and link no daemon code into the frontend module (§4.3). A preliminary check on 2026-11-11, the owner's, made with two AI assistants, found the split sound on that condition. It is not a lawyer's opinion. One is still due before the frontend is distributed. The daemon's ublk transport brings one dependency that is not under a permissive licence: `bitmaps`, MPL-2.0, through `libublk` (found in the review of M1.3b). MPL-2.0 binds changes to that crate's own files and is used unmodified. What it asks of a distribution in that case, by the owner's second check of 2026-11-11 with an AI assistant and by the lead's reading of the licence's text (its §3.2 and §3.3), neither of them a lawyer's opinion: whoever receives the daemon as a binary must be told where the crate's source can be had, the daemon's own licence must not limit their rights in that source, and the crate's notices stay. So every installer bundle and container image that carries the daemon gets a NOTICES file that names each such crate, says that it is under the Mozilla Public License 2.0, and points to its source, with the licence's text beside it. That is a release task and not a design change: it is due with the first binary that leaves the project, and a check that the list of crates under non-permissive licences is complete (`cargo deny` or the like) belongs in CI before then. The lawyer's review covers it with the rest. |

## M2: Cuts and visibility

Deliverables and exit criteria: design §9. The design of a cut is §5.2, of what is uploaded §5.3, of the manifest a published snapshot carries §5.4.

### Status

Planned on 2026-11-14, the day M1 exited. Merged so far: M2.1 (skyzfs/skyzfs#92), with which the daemon recognises uberblock writes and counts cuts, in memory, and shows the count and the newest cut's group in its status; M2.1b (skyzfs/skyzfs#95), with which each cut says whether it opens as itself (status version 12, which also drops the pump's `taken` counters, dead since the log store went), tested against the nine recorded pools; M2.T1 (skyzfs/skyzfs#94), the reference for cuts, the recorded pools and the cut rig; M2.1c (skyzfs/skyzfs#96), with which an uberblock counts only if its slot's checksum holds; M2.T2 (skyzfs/skyzfs#97), object storage and node B on the runners; and M2.T0b (skyzfs/skyzfs#93). M2.2 merged on 2026-11-15 (skyzfs/skyzfs#98, #100, #101): a daemon started with `serve --hold-cuts` holds cuts, copies a block before overwriting it and abandons at the bound; the test-hooks build can write a held cut to a file and release it (status version 13). Nothing is uploaded yet. M2.2c (skyzfs/skyzfs#104) merged the same day: the copies by direct I/O. M2.3 (skyzfs/skyzfs#105, #106) merged the same day: the intent map, kept by a daemon that holds cuts (status version 14). Next: M2.2d (copies in ranges), the 4 MiB default and M2.4 (coding); M2.T3's second stage, the content of held cuts, and M2.T4 (testing).

### Decided before the first package (2026-11-14)

The design left three things to M2's plan. Each is now in the design, by the lead; the owner can overrule any of them.

1. **The instant of a cut, with a pool of writers** (§5.2). The rule asked for requests applied one at a time, and the store has written on eight threads since D6. The instant is now the barrier of the cut's flush: the moment at which every request received before the flush has taken effect and none received after it has begun. The store has had that barrier since M1.3y (a flush waits behind every write on the threads, and everything received after a waiting request waits behind it; overlapping requests take effect in the order received), with a test and a mutant for each clause (`FLUSH_PASSES_WRITES`, `OVERLAP_UNORDERED`). So a cut adds no wait.
2. **An uberblock write counts once the store has applied it**, not once the daemon has acknowledged it (§5.2). The store knows when a write is whole on the device and does not know when the transport's commit of the completion went through. Applied is earlier. A daemon that dies between the two leaves the mark in the cut state; the kernel hands the write to the next instance again, which is a repeat and makes no second cut. The two reasons that make a cut a complete pool hold for it, and the design says why.
3. **The bound on the copies: D7.** At most two cuts are held, the one being uploaded and the newest, and a newer cut replaces the one that waits. The copies of the cut being uploaded have a bound per volume; at the bound that cut is abandoned and its map goes to the next. Writes are never held. What it costs is in D7 and in §5.2: with an uplink too slow to carry a cut within the bound the recovery point does not move, and the status says so.

**The read of these rules (review, 2026-11-14).** The review session read the three decisions and the repeat rule before any package built on them (its findings: `skyzfs-review`, `review/m2-design-read/findings.md`, 27e97ec), and the design changed in six places the same day:

- **A wrong cut at the bound.** The plan freed all copies of an abandoned cut. The newest cut needs those of blocks that did not change between the two instants. Now: a copy is freed only when no held cut needs it (§5.2).
- **A state trusted after the volume failed.** A failed volume is opened again as after a power cut, in the same boot, and the plan would have trusted its cut state. Now the state is not trusted after a failure, and a mark does not outlive the attachment.
- **The waiting cut is replaced when the newer cut's flush has completed**, not at its instant, so that a failed flush leaves the last good cut. With it, the lead's call: a cut whose flush had completed is still uploaded after the volume has failed, by the instance that saw the failure.
- **Half of the barrier had no test.** The review planted "a request may pass a waiting flush" and all 150 tests of `skyzfs-local` passed, plain and with test-hooks. M2.1 adds the test and the mutant.
- **The publish's hand-over needs the snapshot to stay**: a hold from before the publish is asked until the swap (M2.6).
- **A cut whose flush was under way when the daemon died** takes the next start's sync as its flush.

A second look at the amended text (review, `review/m2-design-read/ae850a6.md`, 06e511b) found that the third change had opened a gap in the first: with the replacing moved to the flush's completion, three cuts exist for the length of a flush and writes go on in it. So the map of a cut abandoned in that window goes to the cut that waits and not to the one whose flush is under way (which may fail, and the waiting cut is then uploaded without the map: last upload at 100, cuts at 140, 150 and 151, the bound reached in 151's flush, and the upload of 150 would put the changes of 140 to 150 onto the image at 100); and the copies of a replaced cut are freed by the same rule as at the bound, not all of them. Both are in §5.2 and in M2.2's mutants.

What the read confirmed: the volume at the barrier is a complete pool at *N* under a flush during the uberblock write, stray flushes, a lost daemon with reissue in any order, and repeats; replacing the waiting cut is right as written; the barrier in the code is where the flush executes; a cut's recorded group errs low under multihost and can err high only for a cut made during an import, whose own first flush consumes a surviving mark before an agent can read the count (as the review reads OpenZFS, a writable import syncs a group before it returns; M2.T1's recordings are to confirm it).

Three more rules for the packages, which are the lead's and not the design's:

- **The object formats stay as they are.** A cut is uploaded through `skynbd-engine` as it is (writes into the overlay, a commit, the head ref's compare-and-swap), and a published snapshot is a ref. What a cut must add (its number in the daemon's count, its transaction group, the manifest of §5.4) goes where the formats already have room, such as the ref store's metadata. A package that finds it cannot do without a change to a framed object asks the lead first.
- **New code goes in `skyzfs-*` crates.** The uberblock parser has no I/O and is a crate or a module of its own, so that the rig's reference (M2.T1) and the store's tests can be held against the same recorded pools. The cut state is the store's (`skyzfs-local`). The agent is `crates/skyzfs-agent` and `bins/skyzfs-agent`, modelled on `skynbd-agent`'s durable state record, its recovery planner as a pure function, and its crash matrix.
- **Every rule of §5.2 gets a test that a defect fails**, as the store's rules did in M1 (`Mutants`): the review that found no loss given the rules showed a wrong cut where the record of copies, the handing on of an abandoned map, the rule for clearing an intent bit or the rule on trusting the state was left out, so each of those four is a mutant at least.

### Work packages

Coding packages are numbered M2.n, testing packages M2.Tn. The order below is the order of dependence, not a schedule: M2.T1 and M2.T2 need nothing from the daemon and start at once.

| Package | Needs | What it builds | Done when |
|---|---|---|---|
| **M2.1** Uberblock detection and the count of cuts (skyzfs/skyzfs#92, merged 2026-11-14 at c1dc842) | none | A parser with no I/O: where the four labels and their uberblock rings lie for a volume of a given size (the rigs' pools have their first label at byte 0, the daemon rig's pin `label_at_byte_0`), what in a write is an uberblock write (the magic in either byte order, a transaction group above zero), its transaction group and root block pointer, and what is a repeat. In the store: the mark of an applied uberblock write, the cut at the barrier of the first flush received after it, the count of cuts with each cut's group. Kept in memory in this package. The status file's next version shows the count and the newest cut's group, and drops the fields of the log store that the current one still carries. The request log of the test-hooks build gains what M2.T1's reference needs to place a cut in the daemon's own order (asked by testing on 2026-11-14): an event when the store has applied a write whole, beside the receipt and the commit it has, and the bytes of every write that touches a label. | The parser is held against the on-disk format, cited from OpenZFS's headers, and against the recorded pools of M2.T1 (at least: a create, load, an export and import, a rewind import, `multihost=on`, a stray flush between the uberblock writes and ZFS's flush). Each rule has a test that a defect fails: a request received after a waiting flush that begins before it (the half of the barrier M1 left untested), a repeat counted, a stale multihost write that moves the mark back (coding's finding, 2026-11-14: design §5.2, "What counts as a repeat"), a group of zero counted, a cut counted before the barrier. On the daemon rig, the count and the group in `skyzfsd status` follow the pool's transaction groups. |
| **M2.1b** A cut that opens as itself (skyzfs/skyzfs#95, merged 2026-11-15 at 4b2203e, with M2.1's test against the recorded pools) | M2.1 | The parser gets ZFS's order among uberblocks (`vdev_uberblock_compare`: the group, then the timestamp, then the multihost fields), and the store keeps the uberblocks of the four labels in view: read from the rings when an attachment begins, followed from the writes. Each cut says whether the uberblock that marks it is the one ZFS would choose at its instant. The comparison is between states, not slots: under `multihost=on` a multihost write carries the cut's own group and root with a later timestamp, and is the cut's own state; the cut opens as itself when no uberblock in view of another state is greater than or ties with the greatest of its own. The view is of the volume alone, on purpose: the pool's log device has labels and uberblocks too, and a restore on another node has only the volume. In the status and in the request log's `cut` event. Added on 2026-11-14 on M2.T1's first recordings (design §5.2, "Whether a cut opens as itself"). M2.4 records it with the commit, M2.5 publishes no cut without it, and M2.7's agent syncs the pool past the abandoned groups after an import. | Against M2.T1's rewind recording, the cuts the store says open as themselves are those at which `zdb -e -u` selects the cut's own uberblock, and no others; a mutant that takes the group alone for the order, and one that does not read the rings at the start of an attachment (the abandoned uberblocks were written before the daemon looked); a multihost copy of the cut's state with a later timestamp leaves the cut its own. |
| **M2.1c** An uberblock is one that ZFS would take (skyzfs/skyzfs#96, merged 2026-11-15 at 2c7a3ce) | M2.1b; merges before M2.5 | The parser checks the slot's label checksum (SHA-256, the slot's offset as verifier), for whichever of ZFS's slot sizes verifies, since the store does not know the pool's ashift. Asked for by the review of M2.1 and by coding (2026-11-14). | Bytes in a ring that carry the magic and a high group and fail the checksum make no mark and are not in the view: without the check the cut after them would take that group and say it opens as itself, a recorded group that errs high. A mutant for it. The report says what the check costs for each uberblock write, and that a valid uberblock written by something other than ZFS is beyond it. |
| **M2.2** The cut state (merged 2026-11-15: M2.2a, skyzfs/skyzfs#98 at bf369ca; M2.2b-1, skyzfs/skyzfs#100 at 4331a90; M2.2b-2, skyzfs/skyzfs#101 at 359f952; see "M2.2 as built"; discards are not noted, see "What is open in M2"; two pull requests: M2.2a, the state as logic over a region of bytes with its model test and every mutant but those of trust; M2.2b, the region as a mapped file, trust, the format's version, the status, the two commands and the request log's content) | M2.1 | The map of changed blocks (writes, write-zeroes and discards, by block), frozen at a cut's instant; the copy before overwriting a block of a held cut; at most two held cuts, the replacing of the one that waits, the bound and the abandoning at it (D7); all of it in a file mapped into the daemon, in a versioned format, trusted only in the boot that wrote it and only if no sync of it failed. A state that is not trusted drops its held cuts and counts the whole volume as changed, until M2.3 narrows that. Reading a block of a held cut as it was at the cut's instant, safely beside the write path: the upload will read while writes go on. Two commands in the test-hooks build stand in for the uploader: write a held cut to a file, and release it as uploaded. The request log can carry every write's content, whole or as a hash for each block, so that the reference builds the volume at an instant from the daemon's own record. The status shows the held cuts, the bytes copied, the bound and the count of abandoned cuts. | A property test against a model that keeps every cut whole: over random programs with the daemon lost at every store into the state, each held cut reads as the model's volume at its instant. A mutant for each of: no record of a copy, a copy made after the overwrite, an abandoned map dropped, a replaced cut's map dropped, the abandoned cut's copies all freed (the property test must reach the bound with a cut waiting), the replaced cut's copies all freed (the test must write, while the newer cut's flush runs, a block outside that cut's own map), an abandoned map given to a cut whose flush is under way while another waits (the test must reach the bound in that window and then fail the flush), an abandoned map put in the open map while a cut's flush is under way and none waits (that cut would be uploaded without it, with no failure at all), the waiting cut replaced at the newer one's instant and not at its flush's completion, a freeze that a death can split, a mark recorded before the uberblock write is whole (the test drives a lost daemon whose successor gets the reissued flush before the reissued write; in one instance the flush waits behind the write and the defect shows nowhere), a mark recorded after the completion went to the transport, a mark carried to a device the daemon creates, a state trusted in another boot, a state trusted after a failed sync, a state trusted after the volume failed. The state also holds the newest cut's group and root block pointer, for the repeat rule. The format has a version and a test that refuses another. M2.T3 compares the held cuts with its reference on a real pool, and M2.T4 measures what the state costs the write path against M1's daemon. |
| **M2.2c** The copies by direct I/O (skyzfs/skyzfs#104, merged 2026-11-15 at 76f32ac) | M2.2b-1 | The copies file is written by direct I/O, so that no copy rests on a writeback whose error can be lost ("What is open in M2"). A copy whose write fails lets the cuts go, as now. Also, unless M2.2b-1 took it: the state's file is cut to nothing before it is resized, so that a file shrunk and regrown by two refused opens is not taken up with a held cut reading content from after its instant (review of M2.2b-1, N1; not reachable while the bound is no option). | The model test and mutants still pass; a test in which a copy's write fails at the disk and the cut is let go at that write; M2.T4 has the write path's cost. |
| **M2.2d** Copies in ranges (skyzfs/skyzfs#107, merged 2026-11-17 at e25bf3a) | M2.2c; before M2.4 | As built through M2.2c a copy is made on the queue's thread, one 4 KiB block at a time: a direct read of the image and a direct write into a slot of the copies file for each block, so a 128 KiB write whose blocks all need copies costs 64 synchronous I/Os before it is handed to a thread, and nothing else of the device is dispatched meanwhile (M2.T4 round 1). This package lists the blocks of a write that need copies, reserves slots in runs, and reads and writes each run in one direct I/O; the order of the rules (copy whole, then record, then bit) does not change. Still on the queue's thread. Moving the copy to the I/O thread that makes the write (plan on the queue's thread, copy on a thread, record on the queue's thread, then the write) is M2.2e, decided after a measurement of this one. Two rules M2.2e must keep (review of M2.2d): a freeze must not fall between a write's plan and its record, so the write counts as in flight from its plan and not from its hand-over to a thread, else the new cut finds no record for a block whose content at its instant is that copy and reads the image after the overwrite; and the record step asks again whether each copy is still needed, since a release or another write's abandoning can fall between. | The model test and the mutants pass unchanged; a test counts the I/Os of a write over a held cut; M2.T4 measures it against M2.2c in the regime of round 1 and with releases. |
| **M2.3** The intent map (M2.3a, the map as logic: skyzfs/skyzfs#105, merged 2026-11-15 at 15c661b; M2.3b, the store, the write path and the status: skyzfs/skyzfs#106, merged 2026-11-15 at 89ad21b; see "M2.3 as built") | M2.2 | One bit for a region of the volume, durable before the first write into a region that was clean is handed to the disk; after a restart of the machine the regions it names, and no others, are compared with the newest uploaded cut, or counted as changed whole (the package measures both and says which); a bit is cleared only after the cut that carries the region's changes is committed in object storage and only if nothing in the region changed since that cut's instant. The size of a region is a parameter with a default the package argues from its measurements. | On the simulated disk, with the power cut at every prefix of random programs and the state file left as a power cut leaves it (a well-formed state from seconds earlier included): the next cut holds every block that differs from the newest uploaded cut. Mutants: a write before its bit is durable, a bit cleared before the commit, a bit cleared with a change after the cut's instant, the regions of an untrusted state not carried. What the sync of the map costs on the write path is measured (M2.T4). |
| **M2.4** Upload | M2.2; M2.3 for clearing bits | The engine in the daemon and an uploader off the I/O path. A cut is uploaded only once its flush has completed without error. Its written blocks go in with their content at the instant, its discarded blocks as holes; then the commit and the compare-and-swap of the head ref. The cut's number and group are recorded with the commit, so that an instance that starts after a death knows what the head holds: a death during an upload begins the upload again, and a death between the swap and the state's record of it uploads nothing twice. The upload is fenced by the volume's lease, which the engine has; without the lease or without the object store the volume goes on serving and the cuts are abandoned at the bound. `skyzfsd create` and `serve` learn the object store and the volume's identity there. | The crash matrix: the daemon lost at every PUT, at the swap and at every store into the cut state between them (`FaultStore`'s kill points), and after each the head ref names a commit that is a whole cut, the next upload completes, and no cut holds a block from after its instant. An upload that reads while the write path overwrites: the cut holds the content at its instant (the copy), shown by a test that fails when the read does not look for the copy. The upload syncs the copies file before it commits a cut it read copies for, and abandons the cut if that sync fails (review of M2.2b-1). A cut whose flush failed is never uploaded; a cut whose flush had completed is uploaded by the instance that saw the volume fail, and a read for it that fails abandons it; after a death with a cut's flush under way, the start's sync counts as that flush. M2.T3 imports what was uploaded on node B. |
| **M2.5** Publish in the daemon | M2.4 | How an agent talks to a serving daemon (the package proposes it; the daemon has no control channel today, only its status file). Two requests: the count of cuts, and publish as `snap/t` the first uploaded cut counted after *C* whose group is at or after a given one, with the manifest of §5.4 stored with the ref. A cut that is abandoned hands the publish to the cut that takes its map. A publish belongs to the attachment it began on and is refused or forgotten on another. Asking twice is asking once.
