For M2.6b2: the assist must write a group (a sync task of the agent's own, `zfs set skyzfs:assist=<n>` on the pool's root, then sync; a bare `zpool sync` writes none on an idle pool, M2.T1); report at 3 asks anew with no cut taken and `Abandon(NoProgress)` at 8; look before giving up on `unknown`/`refused rewound`/`expired` (`Done{report: SnapshotGone}`); a file-system model under the record's writes with a crash and an error at each step; the `load_guid` read with a new count equals the one verified with the hold; S1 (premise at Intent), S3 (members by GUID), S4 (monotonic time), S6 (a lock), S11. Checks: 11 of 11 CI runs green, daemon rig 56 of 56 on three images, cut rig on the daemon 15 of 15 and upload 8 of 8.
- **M2.6b2 as built: the agent's engine** (skyzfs/skyzfs#144, merged at 8665d17). The engine drives the planner over fakes of the file system, the pool and the daemon, with a crash and an error at each step of the record's writes (the crash matrix); the record is SZAG v2, amended in place because nothing had released v1. The assist is a sync task of the agent's own (`zfs set skyzfs:assist=<n>` on the pool root, then sync), at twice ASSIST_AFTER after `accepted`, then once per ASSIST_AFTER. Report at 3 asks anew with no cut, `Abandon(NoProgress)` at 8, and AGE_MAX is 2 h (`Why::TooOld`). L5 to L7: a hold is released by the age bound only when no request can still be pending; N4 fixed as L7. Decided at the end (N6): `refused stopped` is an end like `rewound` (look, give up, release the hold, report the code); it goes into M2.6c with the fix of the doc of `ends`. Open for M2.6c: real adapters (`ZfsCli` with timeouts that kill the child, `DaemonSocket`, `skyzfs-agent publish` and `recover`), `skyzfs:assist` out of the manifest's `local`, a cross-restart DoneNotSeen stop and a report sink, S4 (one deadline per client request), S6. Checks: 10 CI runs green on cf53e94, daemon rig 56 of 56 on three images (33 on the stand-in), cut rig on the daemon and upload green, publish rig green.
- **F5 as built: a lease is renewed only where it is still the holder's, in one write** (skyzfs/skyzfs#131, merged at c324c7a; review's F5 of #117). The keeper renewed in two calls, a read and `lease_acquire`, which also steals an expired lease: when B took the lease between A's read and A's take and B's own lease had run out by A's clock, A's take stole it back at a newer epoch and both nodes stopped uploading until a restart (safe, rare). `RefStore::lease_renew(vol, holder, epoch, ttl_ms) -> RenewOutcome` is one conditional write: the row is renewed only where it is `(holder, epoch)`, expired or not (so a store out of reach for longer than the lease does not end the uploads), the epoch is kept (`Renewed`); any other row or none is `NotHeld(row as read)` and nothing is written; it never takes a lease. The memory store applies the rule under its lock, redb in one write transaction, the object store in its read and conditional PUT of the refs object (a take between them changes the etag and the rule is judged again on the row the take wrote). The keeper's renewal is that call: `NotHeld(Some(row))` is lost to that holder and epoch, `NotHeld(None)` lost to nobody, an error unsure; that also closes the open item that a lease lost in the race names no holder. Evidence: the conformance suite on the memory, redb, object and cadenced stores, the race on the object store, review's L4 as a keeper test, 17 plants caught. Not changed (review's P3, N1): two daemons under one holder name both hold the lease, which is why `--holder` must name one daemon only (CLAUDE.md, "The daemon uploads"). The package also carries #127's three small items, #126's y3 (the mirror case of F7) and the fix of Codex's P2 on the channel's connect (non-blocking, retried while the backlog is full, up to the limit).
- **M2.4f as built: the uploader waits after a whole cut is lost** (skyzfs/skyzfs#129, merged at 83899d2; the figures that led to it are in the entry on the first upload under a write load). A cut is lost when its read fails (the store abandoned it at the bound), when the sync of its copies fails, or when the commit stage finds it gone. After a *whole* cut is lost the uploader takes no cut's step for a wait of 60 s, doubling to 900 s (`backoff.rs`, `Backoff::between(WHOLE_FIRST, WHOLE_LAST)`), reset only by a commit that lands (`Uploaded`, `UploadedNotHeld` or `FoundCommitted`); a whole cut read and lost again does not reset it, and the next whole cut after a commit that was a mismatch waits 60 s again, not 900. A *delta* cut lost does not wait (decided: a delta is cheap and its heir is read at once). The state is `WholeLost { why, wait }`; the lease is renewed and publishes step during the wait (the sleep is the earliest of the wait's end, the lease's renewal and a publish due, so no sleep exceeds a third of the lease). A publish can expire during a wait (default expiry 10 minutes, the longest wait 15): one carried by a cut committed before the wait is made during it, one that needs a later cut can expire first, and then the agent asks again (it may ask for up to an hour). Status version 19 (1416 bytes, magic `SKYZST19`; 18 still read): `upload_cuts_lost_whole`, `upload_state` 10 `whole_lost` with `upload_wait_ms` and `upload_why` saying which cut and why. At the cap the leak in run 44412222348's load would be about 2.8 GiB an hour, and a seed begins at most 15 minutes after the load drops. Also in the package, test-only: #125's G3 and G4 (a run of zeros over an empty 64 GiB volume reads the map and the hole set within four passes and costs grow linearly; random slices over several stretches read each block once, as the holes say). **Open for M4's garbage collection** (the segments an attempt PUT stay in the bucket with no commit): a whole cut lost at the bound or at its read, one whose sync failed, one found gone at its commit (that one takes the wait too), a delta lost (no wait, so its segments leave too), and a new instance, which starts with no wait and so may make a first attempt at once. Deleting an attempt's own segments when it is lost (option ii) is not built. Evidence: 31 plants caught, daemon rig 56 PASS per daemon image, cut rig on the daemon 15 of 15 and upload 6 of 6, the zf rerun above.
- **M2.5-2 as built: the daemon's channel** (skyzfs/skyzfs#127, merged at ebf7bd6; the protocol is written in `bins/skyzfsd/src/control.rs`'s module documentation, which is the reference). A Unix stream socket `control` in the volume's run directory, mode 0600, made before `ready` and removed at the end of `serve` (a socket left by a killed instance is replaced by the next, which holds the lock); only the daemon's user and root are answered (`SO_PEERCRED`, others `peer`); one request per connection, the daemon answers and closes; a line is at most 4096 bytes, a whole request (greeting, line, manifest) must be in within 10 s of the accept, each write gets 10 s. Version 1, greeting `skyzfs-control 1`. Requests: `cuts` (answers the attachment nonce A, the cut state S or `none`, the count C of cuts in that state, the newest cut's group G and `opens` = `itself` or `rewind`; the count is read at the store's desk when the request is answered, within 30 s, so a cut counted before the request was sent is in it); `publish name=T attachment=A state=S after=C group=G manifest=N [expiry-s=E]` then N bytes of the ZFS manifest (at most 1 MiB, opaque to the daemon; answers `accepted`, asking twice is asking once; expiry default 600 s, at most 3600, counted from the acceptance on this daemon's uploader, so a restart gives a new deadline); `progress name=T` (`pending`, `done commit=H`, `expired`, `withdrawn`, `unknown`); `withdraw name=T` (ends a pending publish unless made; one whose transaction may have landed is looked up first and can still read `pending` then `done`; the same `publish` asked again undoes a withdrawal while it still pends). The publish is for the first cut of state S counted after C whose group is G or later and that opens as itself, so the agent reads A, S and C with `cuts` before it takes `pool@T`, with G the snapshot's `createtxg`. Refusals are `refused CODE TEXT`: of the channel `version`, `malformed`, `peer`, `no-upload` (the text says why, for example another holder with its epoch), `ended`, `no-answer` (the uploader answers only between two of its steps, limit 30 s; a publish may still have been taken, ask again or withdraw), and of a publish the uploader's refusals (`name`, `stopped` and the others of `skyzfs_upload::Refusal`); `stopped` means ask the next daemon, which looks `snap/T` up, and not "not published". The channel never calls the store from `control()` (the uploader answers between turns; the count goes through the desk with its own limit), a publish is judged against the cut state given at the start of the uploader's run, and a daemon that cannot make its socket serves all the same and says so on stderr. Commands `skyzfsd cuts|publish|publish-progress|withdraw` print the answer line, exit 0 for an answer and 1 for a refusal or none. Status version 18 (1408 bytes; 17 still read): `upload_not_offered`, the publishes (`publish_accepted`, `_published`, `_found`, `_refused`, `_expired`, `_withdrawn`, `_pending`, `_oldest`, `_oldest_ms`, `_last`, `_last_cut`, `_last_group`) and the channel (`control`, `control_answered`, `control_peer`, `control_malformed`). Test hooks: `publish` lines in the request log and kill points `--kill-at-upload manifest:N` (after the Nth framed manifest landed, before its ref's transaction) and `publish:N` (after the Nth publish's transaction, before the uploader knows it). Not done and owed: the rig for the channel (the rewind publish, M2.T3's last stage; testing's), F1's exact form (a cut row naming its commit), retention of `skyzfs/snap` rows (M4). Evidence: 32 plants caught, daemon rig 56 PASS on the three daemon images and 33 on the stand-ins, cut rig on the daemon 15 of 15 on each image and the upload job, control, replay and node B rigs green.
- **The first upload under a write load, found by the benchmark, and what M2.5 is to be** (lead, 2026-11-18). *Finding* (run 44325181328, ubuntu-26.04, one runner, 32 GiB volume, tpcb and select, uploads on to MinIO): in six daemon rows the uploader committed nothing. A state begun on a volume with no commit counts the whole volume as changed, so its first cut is whole; a held whole cut copies the first write to every block after its instant, holes included (a copy of zeros), so the copies (about 23 MB/s, nearly every byte ZFS wrote) fill the 256 MiB bound in about 11 s and the cut is abandoned; the heir of an abandoned whole cut is whole again and starts at block 0. A tpcb window of 123 s abandoned 11 cuts, and about 72 GiB of unreferenced segments stayed in the bucket over four upload rows (M4's garbage collection). A whole upload does not skip holes today: it reads them as zeros and sends zero extents, so they cost reads and turns and no PUTs. *Decided:* M2.4e records which chunks of the image are holes when a state begins; until the state's first cut is committed and let go, a write into such a chunk makes no copy and a whole cut reads such a run as one zero extent. A larger bound and a resumable whole upload are not built. Open on purpose: a volume that is not sparse (`create --zero-fill`, a preallocated or written-through image) has no holes and behaves as before, and a volume with real overwrites during the seed may still not seed. The header-sync pair (`--hold-cuts` with and without `--intent-header-sync`) has no figures until rows that uploaded exist. *M2.5, decided (plan by coding, lead 2026-11-18):* a publish (t, C, G) is carried by the first cut the uploader still knows (number above C, group at or above G, opens as itself) and is its own refs transact right after that cut's commit landed, under the lease fence: CasRef `snap/t` (expected absent) plus a MetaPut `skyzfs/snap/<t>`; SkyNBD's framed manifest and the ZFS manifest (an object of its own, at most 1 MiB, format owned by the agent, versioned in M2.6) are PUT before. Pending publishes are in memory, expire (default 10 minutes, at most 1 hour) and can be withdrawn; a failing publish backs off on its own count and never holds up an upload. The agent's request is the vouch; the daemon keeps no vouch state; a check of the label's vdev tree by the daemon belongs with M4's attach-migration rule. The channel is a Unix socket per volume (protocol in M2.5-2's PR). Open: F1's exact form needs a cut row that names its commit, a new row version read by `tests/kernel/` and `skyzfs-nodeb`, its own package after M2.5-2 and before M2.6 (until then a group check guards, and a rollback to a cut of another state with the same group after `zpool import -T` can pass it); `skyzfs/snap/<t>` rows are not deleted with their refs, which is retention's, M4's; input for M2.6: `:` is allowed in ZFS snapshot names and not in SkyNBD's, `Taken` is the answer after a reboot with a new cut state, and an idle pool makes no next cut, so a publish asked again after a death waits or expires. F5 (renew if mine) is its own package.
- **M2.4's shape, decided before it is built** (lead, 2026-11-15, on the author's plan). Three pull requests: the engine side (segments PUT as they seal, a fault mode for "the swap landed, then the daemon died"), the upload driver as a state machine with its crash matrix, and the daemon (object store and lease options, the uploader's thread, status version 15, the test-hooks release gone). The upload reads a cut on the queue's thread in slices, as the rigs' write command does; reading off that thread is a later package if M2.T4's figures call for it. All-zero blocks go up as zero extents; holes from discards wait for M4. The rigs use MinIO on the runners. **The lease:** a start that cannot reach the object store serves and uploads when it can; a start that finds the lease held by another holder is refused with a status of its own, unless an operator's option says to serve without uploading; a lease lost while serving stops the upload and not the serving, and the status says so. **The daemon uploads every cut whether or not an agent vouched for the pool's layout** (the question left for M2.5): a cut is the volume at an instant in any case, and vouching gates publishing. **The cut's record in object storage needs no format change** (settled the same day). The cut's group goes in Commit v2's existing `group` field, which the engine wrote as none; the cut's identity (a random id of the cut state, the cut's number, its group) goes in a meta row, `skyzfs/cut`, of the refs object, written in the same transact as the head swap, so both land or neither, under the lease. The refs object already has such rows. The smaller shard for fragmented volumes (128 MiB) is a create-time choice: the superblock records the shard size and today's readers honour it. What changes is local: the cut state and the intent map's header each get the state's id, with new versions of their own. **Settle later, not at the start** (Q1, lead, 2026-11-17, replacing the first plan, which had serve read head's row and wait a bounded time for the store before the open). The open needs no object store: the assumed base is the intent map's base (the newest committed cut it names: state id, number, group), and a start that does not take up the cut state counts the map's regions as M2.3 does. Before each cut the uploader reads head's row and checks it in this order. (1) The row is the cut being uploaded: the commit had landed and the daemon died before the release; release the cut, upload nothing. (2) A cut that holds part of the volume: the row is the assumed base and head's commit is the row's (its group is the row's group); upload against it. (3) A cut that holds the whole volume (the first cut when the intent map was begun anew, or the first after a mismatch; Q6; a start that takes the map up after an acknowledged failure is not this case: its first cut is a delta onto the map's base by rule 2, as the rig showed on node B, 2026-11-18): head's commit is the row's whatever the row names, or with no row head's commit has no group; upload it, since a whole cut needs no base. (4) Anything else is a mismatch (no row, another cut, another state, a head that another writer moved): every held cut is abandoned and counted, the whole volume counts as changed in the open epoch, and the next cut is whole. The uploader never moves head backwards on its own. The commit guards the row with a MetaCas in the head swap's transact, expecting the row the check read or its absence, so a row that moved between the check and the commit fails the swap; this holds for a whole cut too. The status says unchecked, matched or mismatched, with the reason and the count of abandoned cuts, and which rule each uploaded cut went under (M2.4c). An unknown store at a start therefore costs nothing; only a real mismatch costs a whole upload. Open, listed on purpose: a row of the same state with a higher number than the map's base (our own later commit that the map missed after a power cut) stays a mismatch (Q8); a bound that abandons the cut being uploaded after its commit landed and before its release is answered in M2.4b-2 by a call that names a committed cut no longer held (P1); after a mismatch on an idle pool nothing is uploaded until the pool writes its next group, since the daemon makes no cut of its own (N4); while the map's file refuses writes, the instance that saw a failure cannot let its cut go, so it uploads no later cut (F1, left to M2.5 with design §5.2's cut held across a failure). **As built in M2.4b-2** (`crates/skyzfs-upload`, the driver as a state machine over a small engine trait, with a crash matrix of 70 kill points on object storage and 21 in the cut state): P1 is answered by a store call that names a committed cut no longer held, so the next cut is a delta onto it. The driver stops for good only when the store keeps a committed cut because the intent map could not be written (F1/F2); other failures upload as before. Also open, from the review: the head is matched by group alone, so after a rewind import two commits can share a group (N1, Q5 as decided); segments left by a given-up upload are for M4's garbage collection, which must spare segments an upload under way has put early (N3). **Decided for M2.4c** (lead, 2026-11-18, G7): the daemon acquires the lease at start and acquiring creates the lease row if absent, so a fencing store with no row is never an upload target (no lease, no upload; the status says why); a conflict that is given up puts the uploader in a backoff, doubling from 1 s to 5 min and reset on a success, and the status says the commit was refused and when it retries, so a whole cut is not re-uploaded every cycle.
- **Carried from M1** and not M2's to close: the ZFS that made no progress after a suspension on arm64, the benchmark row that hung once, the flush cost on real flash (design §10).

## M3 to M9

No work package has started. Their design is in the design document: restore and clone in §5.4, the frontend for macOS and Windows in §4.3, streaming the intent log in §5.5. Each milestone gets its section here when its first package is planned.

## Review record

Every independent read of the design and every review of a work package, with what it changed.

| Date | Scope | Outcome |
|---|---|---|
| 2026-11-10 | §4.1, §5.2, §4.3 and §5.4, an independent read before any code | The first draft allowed a daemon that passed its tests and corrupted a pool, in two ways: it treated daemon loss as a power cut, and it left discards outside the write order. Both are fixed in §4.1, with an error model, limits, a crash model and a definition of a cut as a log position. §5.3 now keeps cut-time block versions. Left for CI to pin on the runners: flush delivery on ublk, the recovery flags, and where `zpool create` puts the labels. |
| 2026-11-10 | The revised §4.1, §5.2 and §5.3, a second independent read | The cut argument holds against concurrent writes, deferred frees and TRIM, checked in the source, and no longer leans on ZFS's flush before the uberblocks. Fixed: overlapping in-flight operations had been allowed to mix per block, which no log order can produce; a repeat uberblock was defined by transaction group alone, which could suppress a real cut after a rewind; flush coverage and the failed state were undefined across a daemon restart; reads had no place in the order. Not yet checked: the 2.4 port branches, only upstream master. |
| 2026-11-10 | The whole document, automated review on the design pull request (skyzfs/skyzfs#2) | Read-only browsing had been described as an import of the snapshot under its original GUID. ZFS refuses a second import of an open GUID even read-only, so browsing is now a throwaway clone with a new GUID, or `.zfs/snapshot` on the node that holds the pool (§5.4). The write log's flush on macOS had been given as `F_FULLFSYNC` or `F_BARRIERFSYNC`. The second does not persist, so it cannot meet §4.1 (§5.1). A read that could not reach object storage had been failed after a bound, on the assumption that `failmode=wait` would then suspend the pool. ZFS passes an ordinary read error to the application instead, so such reads are now held (§4.1). |
| 2026-11-11 | §4.2, how the daemon keeps and gives up a ublk attachment, an independent read before any code, against OpenZFS 2.4.1, Linux 7.0 and the `libublk` source | The first version let ZFS resume on a shorter log in two ways. If the failing disk also refused the log's marker, the next instance saw a healthy volume and no device, added one under the old name, and `zpool clear` carried on. And an operator could acknowledge a failure while the pool was still suspended. Fixed: a failure note in `/run` that does not depend on the failing disk, written before the device is deleted and before the marker is attempted, and no second service in the same boot of a volume a client held when it failed. Also fixed: nothing stopped a second daemon from recovering a log under a live writer (a lock on the volume's directory); the identity check rested on a file the crate keeps and now uses a nonce; the crate deletes a device when its handle is dropped, which would have taken the device from under a pool at every orderly exit. Corrected: the daemon cannot deliver `ENXIO` on Linux, and ZFS raises it itself once its probe fails. Not established: what a scrub in progress records at the failure, and whether the event daemon reopens a device that reappears. |
| 2026-11-11 | §4.3 and §5.4, review on the design pull request (skyzfs/skyzfs#2), checked against both ports' sources | Two errors. The frontend's path had the form `user:<name>`, which both ports' disk vdevs reject as not absolute before any provider is asked, so the hook would never have been reached; the path is now `/dev/udrive_shm/<name>`. And a clone made on the node that holds the original could have opened the original's log device: the copied configuration names it, an import that allows a missing log still opens one that is present, and the offline GUID change writes labels. The offline session is now isolated so that it can open the clone only, and the agent writes every configuration ZFS sees. Checking the second finding showed a third error: ZFS refuses to change the GUID of a pool whose log device is missing, so the log device is now removed first, in the same session. The same review found no counterexample to the cut rule of §5.2 in either port's source for a single data vdev; that is a reading, not a test. |
| 2026-11-11 | §4.2, the check of what was acknowledged, an independent read against the store's code, with the kernel's behaviour from its sources and history | The rule closes the hole it was written for. As first worded it could miss in two ways and raise a false alarm in two. Fixed: the position never moves back, across queues and across instances; positions are compared and not records, so a log reclaimed by a checkpoint passes; the start of the record is stored, clear of padding at a change of segment; the store of the position cannot tear and cannot be skipped; an error from the recovery's sync counts as a failed check. Added by the lead on reading it again: the position belongs to the volume and the boot, so a detach and a new attachment are checked as well; the reviewer then found that this needs the position bound to the store's own identity, or a volume made again under the same name raises a false alarm. The check works only because recovery validates from the device. Building it then showed that both the lead's wording and the read had compared the wrong end: the log's, which recovery's own seal moves, and not the valid prefix's. Not measured: that the kernel drops the pages it could not write. |
| 2026-11-11 | §4.2, the library, after a spike on the kernel (M1.3b) | The reading held for the queue: completion from another thread, the idle time-out, take-over with reissue under load. It did not hold for the control path, in two ways that only a run showed: every failed command reads as "not supported", and a failed add deletes whatever device holds the number. The daemon now sends add, lookup and delete itself. The four device states the start-up rules assumed are three in the driver. Open: whether the library's control object can start a device it did not add (read, not run). |
| 2026-11-11 | M1.3a (skyzfs/skyzfs#10), the daemon without the kernel, reviewed against §4.2 | The order of steps holds against every kill and every stranger the reviewer could construct, 20,000 random chains included. Two races it does not hold against, both in the text as much as in the code: `detach` acted without the volume's lock, so a daemon starting beside it could lose its device or its record; and the acknowledged position was read before the lock, so it could be older than what a still-serving instance went on to acknowledge. Fixed in the text: everything that changes the attachment is done under the lock, and the lock is a step before the position is read. |
| 2026-11-11 | §4.2, the attachment and the failed volume, against the first kernel rounds of the daemon rig (M1.T4, a stand-in server, one run per image) and a test in M1.3 that kills a start after every step | Measured what had only been read: the pool suspends and applications block without an error; a kept device resumes after `zpool clear`, from ZFS's memory. New: a deleted device never came back, its number stays taken while the pool holds it, and the plain delete waits for that, so the delete is asynchronous. The test found that a crash between a refused add and the next record left the record naming a stranger's device and every later start refused; and that a note left behind a deleted device would vouch for the next program given its number. Fixed: taken numbers are passed over before a record is written; whose device it is goes by the note first and the kernel's values second, in every state; a note is removed before its device is deleted. The lead's first layout had one acknowledged position per run directory, which two volumes on a machine would have shared; per-volume files now have their own subdirectory. A later round: neither `zpool export -f` nor `zpool destroy -f` removes the suspended pool; `fsync` and `syncfs` of other file systems are not held up by it, and the `sync` that hung was the `sync FILE` of 26.04's coreutils. Later rounds added: unmounting first does not help; zed changes nothing and floods; a `sync()` of everything hangs too; and, on a kept device served again from an older image, `zpool clear` resumed the pool with acknowledged files lost, which is the case the refusal is for. Not measured: any of it on `skyzfsd` itself. |
| 2026-11-11 | §4.2, every sentence about the driver or the binding, read by the testing session for what a kernel check can pin (plan of M1.T6) | Nineteen pins proposed, and five places where the text could not be pinned as it stood: the wait for a device that is still live had no number and an outdated measurement; one sentence still called the flags an open question that another had settled; the states were named and their transitions nowhere listed; the step at which the kernel refuses a second server was not named; what `detach` can know of a holder was still put as a question. All five fixed in the text; the transitions are now a table that says for each row whether a run or a reading stands behind it. |
| 2026-11-11 | §5.1, the first run of `skyzfsd` through the kernel rig (M1.3b, before review) | Every check of the rig that judges the daemon passed on both images. It also showed a gap in this document: §5.1 said what a checkpoint is and never when one happens, the daemon therefore called none, and the take-over time grew with the log from under 3 s to 23 s over twenty kills. The rule for when the daemon checkpoints is now in §5.1; building it is work package M1.3c. |
| 2026-11-11 | §4.2, the failed volume's delete, against the control checks of the kernel rig (M1.T7, a test server, one run per image) and the driver's source | The paragraph said that deleting the device fails every request in flight. It does not fail one that the server holds: the delete waits for it, and every add and delete of a ublk device on the node waits behind the delete. `skyzfsd` as built in M1.3b deletes its device while its pump may hold requests that a hanging store call never completes. Recorded as a finding on skyzfs/skyzfs#15 and not as a reason to hold it; the fix is work package M1.3d, in which a failed volume answers what it holds before it deletes. |
| 2026-11-11 | §4.2, the daemon as built (M1.3b), reviewed on the code by the review session and by Codex, and against runs with defects put back | The largest finding was in memory and not in logic: the request buffers were freed at the queue's drop while the driver still held their addresses, and the kernel test written to catch that passed with the defect in place. The property is now shown by counting deallocations, and the damage was shown once on purpose. Also found and fixed: the last commits of a stop not handed over, a device with larger requests than the buffers, a create into a directory with other files, the lock on a file the name no longer names, a run directory that lands on a pool through a path that did not resolve, and a deleted device counted as held because another volume had taken its number. Two kernel tests asserted more than the driver promises and were changed, each after one failing run, with the run named. One gap is carried and not fixed: a failed volume can hold requests when its device is deleted (M1.3d). |
| 2026-11-11 | §4.2, what a pool does after a refused start, from the testing session's observation on the daemon (one run per image) | "ZFS finds the disk dead when it checks" had been measured only with a reader running. With writes alone the pool stayed ONLINE over a deleted device for more than five minutes and kept acknowledging synchronous writes from its log device. Written down as measured, with the cause open: a paragraph in §4.2 and a risk in §10. The aim "the pool is told within a bound" becomes part of M1.3d's acceptance; the rig is to record where ZFS stands in that window (M1.T7). |
| 2026-11-11 | §4.2, the same observation with the pool's transaction groups, events, thread stacks and error counters recorded, and a pool without a log device; read against `zio_done` in OpenZFS 2.4.1 | The cause is in ZFS and by design: failed writes are executed again without end, and only a failed read suspends the pool. The log device is what keeps acknowledging. Decided: the daemon reports the state and the agent makes the pool read (M2); the daemon does not act on a pool. |
| 2026-11-11 | §5.1, the rule of the marks as built (M1.3c, skyzfs/skyzfs#17), reviewed on the code by the review session and by Codex, and run on a kernel by hand | The rule held as designed. Found in review and fixed: discards were not counted in a step's bound, so one step could take a whole log of them; a hold released on an idle volume left the worker asleep over a long log; the status was one write behind on an idle volume and said a log of 0 after a start; and three tests assumed how the worker is scheduled, one of which failed only in the slower coverage build. On a kernel the log stayed at the high mark under load and the start no longer grows with the kills. Not shown there yet: a kill inside a step, and the suite with low marks. And one gap in the rule itself, found by Codex at the end: write-zeroes records at the log's start let the log outgrow the high mark (M1.3e). |
| 2026-11-11 | §5.1, the log's bound when a step takes less than a request adds (M1.3e, skyzfs/skyzfs#19) | At the high mark the worker steps until the log is below it before the next request; the cost behind write-zeroes records measured (131 s and 81 s for 128 MiB, one run per image); write-zeroes is to deallocate instead of writing zeros (M1.3f, not built) |
| 2026-11-11 | §4.2, a failed volume holds no request (M1.3d, skyzfs/skyzfs#18), reviewed on the code by the review session and by Codex | Built as designed. Rule changed: a start that refuses an unacknowledged failure deletes the volume's waiting device. Found in review and fixed: a delete the driver refused left a device no later start would delete (the note is put back); the failure note is written before the wait. Left open: the two waits without a bound (§10) |
| 2026-11-12 | §5.1, a write-zeroes record deallocates (M1.3f, skyzfs/skyzfs#21), reviewed on the code by the review session and by Codex | Built as designed. Found in review and fixed: a hole that ends inside a file-system block larger than the write unit rewrote blocks with no preimage (the store now deallocates only where the block is no larger; the same exposure of every write is older and is M1.3g); a refusal in the middle of a step let the step write far more than its bound (the step ends there); an interrupted call is made again. Left open: the calls of macOS and Windows; the first step after an open |
| 2026-11-12 | §5.1, the write unit against the file system's block and the kernel's page (M1.3g, skyzfs/skyzfs#23), reviewed on the code by the lead, the review session and Codex | Built as designed. Found in review and fixed: an open did not compare the page size of the kernel it runs under; an open asked every entry of the directory, so a subdirectory beside the store refused a healthy volume (shown on real files by the review session); the test of the exposure showed only the log's loss, and with the log left out its workload lost nothing (widened; it now shows the home image's loss too). | 2026-11-13 | Decision D6, direct I/O on the image (design §4.2, §5.1, §9, §10), by the review session, against the figures and against what direct I/O gives on Linux | The decision is carried by the figures; the text was not, and is corrected. Direct I/O does not add survival of the daemon's death, which the page cache gave as well; it adds that no acknowledged write waits in a cache a failed sync can drop unwritten. Over a hole or an unwritten extent a returned write needs the file system's commit to survive a power cut (the extent split shown on ext4 by the review; the power-cut half read, not measured), which the contract allows and `fallocate` alone does not close. The start's sync carries more under direct I/O. Three claims said more than the table: the fast-sync jobs (three, four rows), the cache's share of the bounded loss (8 to 10 points in one job, not 17%), and eight threads as the default (slow-sync evidence only). A file system that buffers behind direct I/O cannot be caught by the start's check. |

## References

[^t9]: The benchmark's topology `zfs-skyzfsd` (skyzfs/skyzfs#24): the pool's data device served by a release build of `skyzfsd` with its default arguments, the same pool image, datasets and separate log device as `zfs-slog`. pgbench tpcb-like at scale 100, 8 clients, 30 s of warm-up and 120 s measured, postgres in a 1 GiB memory cgroup, the ARC limited to 768 MiB, a pool of 24 GiB, origin delay 0. Each run is one runner and each row was measured once in it. The daemon as built, with M1.3f: <https://github.com/skyzfs/skyzfs/actions/runs/43143907710>, <https://github.com/skyzfs/skyzfs/actions/runs/43143912986>, <https://github.com/skyzfs/skyzfs/actions/runs/43143917908>, <https://github.com/skyzfs/skyzfs/actions/runs/43143923434>; before M1.3f: <https://github.com/skyzfs/skyzfs/actions/runs/43139599024>. With a bounded row, the daemon in a memory cgroup of 1 GiB beside an unbounded row: <https://github.com/skyzfs/skyzfs/actions/runs/43144256861>, <https://github.com/skyzfs/skyzfs/actions/runs/43148429064>, <https://github.com/skyzfs/skyzfs/actions/runs/43148434483>, <https://github.com/skyzfs/skyzfs/actions/runs/43148439019>. The 1 GiB is a choice, and a cgroup limit throttles the daemon's write-back, which is not what direct I/O would cost. The figures were read from the runs' logs by the testing session and checked against the logs by the review session, all 26 ratios; the lead looked at two runs. The runs' JSON was written before the tool recorded each row's machine and origin delay, so re-rendering it prints no verdict; the ratios stand in the logs of the runs' combined-table jobs.
[^t10]: The benchmark keeps, for each daemon row, the difference of every `time_*` number between two readings of `skyzfsd status` on either side of the measured window, 122.7 to 123.0 s apart for 120 s of pgbench (skyzfs/skyzfs#28). Same settings as in the note before; the daemon is a release build of `zfs-topology` at 58e1b54. Each run is one runner and each row was measured once in it; ten runs, the last two (<https://github.com/skyzfs/skyzfs/actions/runs/43173412352>, slow sync, the model at 4,000 tps and the daemon at 0.895 and 0.926 of it; <https://github.com/skyzfs/skyzfs/actions/runs/43173407710>, fast sync) after the decision was written. Of the first eight, on the slower runners: <https://github.com/skyzfs/skyzfs/actions/runs/43166011944>, <https://github.com/skyzfs/skyzfs/actions/runs/43166016571> (the one whose syncs are slow: the home image's 42 to 46 ms per step, a read's wait 696 to 755 µs, four fifths of it the step; its daemon rows were at 1.02 and 1.07 of a model row of 3,124 tps), <https://github.com/skyzfs/skyzfs/actions/runs/43166021441>, <https://github.com/skyzfs/skyzfs/actions/runs/43166026162>, <https://github.com/skyzfs/skyzfs/actions/runs/43169898732>. On the faster: <https://github.com/skyzfs/skyzfs/actions/runs/43169903002>, <https://github.com/skyzfs/skyzfs/actions/runs/43169894572>, <https://github.com/skyzfs/skyzfs/actions/runs/43169906456>. Three of the eight have a row bounded to 1 GiB. The sums of each kind's wait by cause equal its wait exactly in every row; the worker's activities equal the window to within one open span and the file's age at each end, about 0.1%. The figures were read by the testing session from the runs' summaries.
[^lbsprobe]: A probe by the testing session with `skyzfsd` of skyzfs/skyzfs#23 at 06b77f9, built without hooks, one run per image (kernels 7.0.0-1017 and 7.0.0-1019, pages of 4096 bytes, mkfs.xfs 6.6.0 and 6.18.0): <https://github.com/skyzfs/skyzfs/actions/runs/43144717671>. `mkfs.xfs -b size=16k` on a loop device; default ext4 and default XFS as controls, both reporting 4096. The probe records and judges nothing. The comparison with the page size and the narrowing to the store's own files were added in review, after this run: they rest on unit tests and, for a stray entry, a test on real files. That the volume made there has a unit of 16384 is shown only by its being served where the copy was refused: the daemon printed no unit at that commit. Read by the lead from the run's artifacts; the reading is on the pull request.
[^zfserr]: `module/zfs/zio.c`: a flush that returns `ENOTSUP` sets `vdev_nowritecache`, and `zio_flush` is issued with `ZIO_FLAG_DONT_PROPAGATE`. `module/zfs/vdev_label.c`: `vdev_config_sync` discards the result of the flush before the labels, and `vdev_uberblock_sync_list` that of the flush after the uberblocks. Read in upstream master on 2026-11-10. Observed once on ZFS 2.4.1 as packaged for Ubuntu 26.04: with a fault injected into every flush, `zpool sync` returned success, the pool stayed online with no errors counted, and ZFS went on issuing flushes[^probe].
[^libublk]: `libublk`, <https://github.com/ublk-org/libublk-rs>, version 0.4.8 as published on crates.io, MIT or Apache-2.0. Read on 2026-11-11: `src/ctrl.rs` (recovery, quiesce; `ublk_ctrl_need_retry` and `ublk_ctrl_cmd`, which return the result of a retry with the legacy opcode; `Drop for UblkCtrlInner`, which deletes the device of an object made for an add and not disowned), `src/io.rs` (queue handling, completion from another context, the buffer's address given at the fetch and at each completion), and the build script of `libublk-rs-sys`.
[^spike]: `docs/skyzfs-ublk-spike.md`, part of work package M1.3b (skyzfs/skyzfs#15): a server over a file, driven by `fio` with verification and no pool, on GitHub's Ubuntu 24.04 and 26.04 images (kernel 7.0), runs <https://github.com/skyzfs/skyzfs/actions/runs/42997706015> and <https://github.com/skyzfs/skyzfs/actions/runs/42999759799>. Two runs per image for most answers, one for the kills under load and for the driver's own error codes. That the library's control object can start a device the daemon added itself was read from its source then; the binding's tests have since shown it[^bind].
[^bind]: The kernel tests of the binding (`crates/skyzfs-ublk-sys/tests/kernel.rs`) and `skyzfsd` under the daemon rig, both from work package M1.3b (skyzfs/skyzfs#15; its description has every figure with its run), as root on GitHub's Ubuntu 24.04 and 26.04 images (kernel 7.0), one runner per image. The binding's tests: <https://github.com/skyzfs/skyzfs/actions/runs/43013227075> (an add reports a dead device; a second add is refused as taken and changes nothing; the asynchronous delete frees the number; the library's control object made with no flag sets the parameters and starts a device the binding added; dropping the library's objects deletes nothing; `/run/ublksrvd` is not created) and <https://github.com/skyzfs/skyzfs/actions/runs/43028536432> (also: writes of 4 KiB and of 1 MiB arrive whole in one life and across a take-over, with one first-write copy in each life at a depth of 8; a take-over beside another server's files under `/run/ublksrvd` leaves them unchanged; the begin of a recovery on a device that was never started is answered as busy). One run per image for each. The daemon under the rig: <https://github.com/skyzfs/skyzfs/actions/runs/43013952798>, <https://github.com/skyzfs/skyzfs/actions/runs/43023027664> and <https://github.com/skyzfs/skyzfs/actions/runs/43028536432>.
[^wberr]: Linux records a write-back error on the file's in-memory inode and returns it from the next `fsync` of each open file that has not seen it; a `write` does not return it. Since 4.17 an error nobody has seen is also reported once to a file opened afterwards ("errseq: Always report a writeback error once", <https://lkml.iu.edu/hypermail/linux/kernel/1804.3/06625.html>). The record is lost if the inode is evicted first, and file systems usually leave the pages that could not be written marked clean, so they too can be dropped and a later read returns what the disk holds (LWN, "PostgreSQL's fsync() surprise", 2018, <https://lwn.net/Articles/752063/>). Read on 2026-11-11, not measured.
[^ublkdrv]: Linux v7.0, `drivers/block/ublk_drv.c`: a server's result becomes a block status through `errno_to_blk_status`, which has no entry for `ENXIO`; `SET_PARAMS` is refused once the device has been started; stopping a device aborts its queued requests and deletes the disk; ending a recovery makes the device live and only then requeues what was waiting; beginning one answers `EBUSY` unless the device waits and its former server's handle is released (`ublk_ctrl_start_recovery`); the request time-out only re-arms itself for a device that was not added unprivileged (`ublk_timeout`). OpenZFS 2.4.1, `include/os/linux/kernel/linux/blkdev_compat.h`, `bi_status_to_errno`: no status maps to `ENXIO`; `module/os/linux/zfs/vdev_disk.c`, `vdev_disk_io_done`: on `EIO` the disk is checked and a dead one is marked for removal; `module/zfs/vdev.c`, `vdev_probe_done`: a failed probe makes the vdev inaccessible. Read on 2026-11-11, not run. Also in `ublk_drv.c`: a device's number is freed in `ublk_cdev_rel`, when the last reference to the device is dropped, and the disk holds one from `START_DEV` until `ublk_free_disk`, that is until its last opener closes. Until then an add on that number fails with `EEXIST` (`ublk_alloc_dev_number`), and `DEL_DEV` waits for the number to be freed where `DEL_DEV_ASYNC` does not (`ublk_ctrl_del_dev`). The rig then showed both[^failrig].
[^probe]: Workflow `skyzfs-kernel-probe`, runs of 2026-11-10: <https://github.com/skyzfs/skyzfs/actions/runs/42923293696> and, for the repeated kills, <https://github.com/skyzfs/skyzfs/actions/runs/42925564790>. The probe is `tests/kernel/ublk-probe.sh` on the `zfs-topology` branch, and the benchmark rows are `zfs-ublk` and `zfs-loop` in `tests/bench/topology-bench.sh` (runs 42919169910, 42919174435 and 42924784572).
[^replay]: Workflow `skyzfs-replay-rig`, runs of 2026-11-11 on Ubuntu 26.04 (kernel 7.0, ZFS module 2.4.1): <https://github.com/skyzfs/skyzfs/actions/runs/42928531993>, <https://github.com/skyzfs/skyzfs/actions/runs/42932034365>, <https://github.com/skyzfs/skyzfs/actions/runs/42935788757> and <https://github.com/skyzfs/skyzfs/actions/runs/42939816010>. The buffers: with the queue's buffers freed at its drop, <https://github.com/skyzfs/skyzfs/actions/runs/43073520161> (freed memory reused under the thread's blocks, four writes in flight, no byte changed, and the allocator's count: 400 deallocations at 25 of 25 stops), <https://github.com/skyzfs/skyzfs/actions/runs/43076882940> (writers held until the memory was taken: glibc's `corrupted size vs. prev_size` at the first such stop, Ubuntu 24.04) and <https://github.com/skyzfs/skyzfs/actions/runs/43078737004> (the test as it is now: it ends at the first stop, on the count). With the buffers kept, three runs on both images, the last at the merged head: <https://github.com/skyzfs/skyzfs/actions/runs/43082993738>: the binding's ten kernel tests pass, no deallocation at any of 25 stops, and in 12 of 12 stops with the writers held, writes were issued after the memory was taken and no byte changed. The deletes: 40 of each kind per image and run in the binding's delete test, five runs; still there at the first look once (26.04, <https://github.com/skyzfs/skyzfs/actions/runs/43080463343>), longest time to gone 31 ms. "Taken" after a lookup had found nothing: once as a failure of the test as first written (<https://github.com/skyzfs/skyzfs/actions/runs/43079066283>, 26.04), then, counted per 39 adds, 2 and 0 on 26.04 and 0 and 0 on 24.04 in the binding's own runs, and 1 on each image in the control rig of M1.T7 (<https://github.com/skyzfs/skyzfs/actions/runs/43085579233>). The whole rig on the daemon at the merged code: <https://github.com/skyzfs/skyzfs/actions/runs/43086281904>; the run before it on the daemon, checks a, b and d: <https://github.com/skyzfs/skyzfs/actions/runs/43064604469>. One runner per image and run.

[^delwait]: The control checks of the kernel rig, work package M1.T7 (merged as skyzfs/skyzfs#20), Ubuntu 24.04 and 26.04, kernels 7.0.0-1017 and 7.0.0-1019, one run per image and one measurement per figure: <https://github.com/skyzfs/skyzfs/actions/runs/43073076409> (holds of 20 s and 60 s) and <https://github.com/skyzfs/skyzfs/actions/runs/43074927907> (the other commands timed while the delete waits, and the server killed); the time for the number to come free is from <https://github.com/skyzfs/skyzfs/actions/runs/43070686788>. The driver's source reads the same way, in a tree newer than those kernels: `ublk_ctrl_del_dev` takes the driver's one control mutex, which `ublk_ctrl_add_dev` also takes, and calls `ublk_remove` before it looks at whether to wait for the number; for a device with user recovery that does not fail I/O, `ublk_stop_dev` holds the device's mutex and loops in `ublk_wait_tagset_rqs_idle` until no started request is left; a device that is not live skips the loop.

A job is slow-sync by its disk-sync line (141 to 208 MiB/s for a buffered `fdatasync` of 64 MiB against 3,000 to 4,300 on the others), and before that line existed by the home image's sync per step (37 to 39 ms against 3.6 to 3.8).
[^zioreexec]: OpenZFS 2.4.1, `module/zfs/zio.c`, `zio_done`: for a logical zio with an error, one that is allocating and has not `ZIO_FLAG_CANFAIL` is marked for re-execution unless the error is `ENOSPC`, in which case the pool is suspended; a read or a free that fails with `ENXIO` suspends the pool unless `failmode` is `continue`; and any other zio that may not fail and is not marked for re-execution suspends it. Each re-executed write posts an `ereport.fs.zfs.data` event, which is the stream the rig counted.
[^failrig]: `tests/kernel/daemon-rig.sh` and `failed-volume-obs.sh`, workflows `skyzfs-daemon-rig` and `skyzfs-failed-volume`, work package M1.T4 (skyzfs/skyzfs#11), whose description has every figure with its run. Runs of 2026-11-11 on GitHub's Ubuntu 24.04 (kernel 7.0.0-1017-azure) and 26.04 (7.0.0-1019-azure) images, ZFS 2.4.1, each image on its own runner, one run per image: the daemon rig, <https://github.com/skyzfs/skyzfs/actions/runs/42972177917> and <https://github.com/skyzfs/skyzfs/actions/runs/42980469527>; a device deleted under an imported pool, <https://github.com/skyzfs/skyzfs/actions/runs/42971643028>; syncing beside a suspended pool, and `zpool export -f` and `zpool destroy -f` on one, <https://github.com/skyzfs/skyzfs/actions/runs/42975781582> (26.04 only), <https://github.com/skyzfs/skyzfs/actions/runs/42979323211>, <https://github.com/skyzfs/skyzfs/actions/runs/42985731155> (zed, `sync()` of everything) and <https://github.com/skyzfs/skyzfs/actions/runs/42986104286> (unmounting first); the green runs of the whole daemon rig, with the older-content observation, <https://github.com/skyzfs/skyzfs/actions/runs/42988261841> and <https://github.com/skyzfs/skyzfs/actions/runs/42992148479>. On the 24.04 image the ZFS commands are 2.2.2 beside the 2.4.1 kernel module. The server is a stand-in for `skyzfsd` built around the ublk project's reference server. Several of these runs ended red for faults of the rig that were then fixed, and one was cancelled part way; the figures quoted are from the checks that ran. The review of the rig checked the figures of the kill test, the held read, the kept device and the refusals against one image's logs, and corrected one: the rig's first figure for how long a killed server's device stays live was the length of one look, not that time.
[^liverig]: The daemon rig's watcher, work package M1.T5 (skyzfs/skyzfs#12): during each of 20 kills of a stand-in server under a pool's load it looks at the device without pause and records when the last look that answered live began and when the first that did not began. The first figure is a lower bound that a slow look cannot inflate, since the state moves one way only; the second can be inflated. Runs of 2026-11-11, one runner per image: <https://github.com/skyzfs/skyzfs/actions/runs/42997095276>, <https://github.com/skyzfs/skyzfs/actions/runs/43006180589>, <https://github.com/skyzfs/skyzfs/actions/runs/43006577372> and <https://github.com/skyzfs/skyzfs/actions/runs/43012202732>. The same package's runs have the scrub at a failure (three runs per image) and the pool that nothing touched after it suspended (<https://github.com/skyzfs/skyzfs/actions/runs/42997101770>). The spike's own figure, without a pool, was 4 to 54 ms on an idle device and 6 to 17 ms under load, three kills per image each[^spike].
[^waitrig]: The daemon rig, work package M1.T6 (skyzfs/skyzfs#13, whose description has every figure with its run), on a stand-in server, GitHub's Ubuntu 24.04 and 26.04 images, one runner per image. The waiting device: <https://github.com/skyzfs/skyzfs/actions/runs/43012676160>, <https://github.com/skyzfs/skyzfs/actions/runs/43016426113> and <https://github.com/skyzfs/skyzfs/actions/runs/43022806023>; the kernel's limit of ten hung-task reports per boot was lifted for the run, so the reports' presence means something. The same runs have the kills with the delayed acknowledgement. The run on the code as merged, after review: <https://github.com/skyzfs/skyzfs/actions/runs/43031173395> (the device waiting at all 30 looks in 300 s on both images; 28 and 29 requests found applied and not acknowledged over ten take-overs under a pool, 13 and 12 over the 29 of the suite's pass).
[^ckpt]: The daemon rig on `skyzfsd` itself for the first time, work package M1.3b before its review, Ubuntu 26.04 and 24.04, one run per image: <https://github.com/skyzfs/skyzfs/actions/runs/43013952798>. Twenty kills of the daemon under a pool's write load, each taken over by a new daemon: the time until the device was served again was 2.8 s after the first kill and 23.4 s after the twentieth on 26.04, growing at nearly every kill; the reference server stays between one and two seconds. A second run, in which the daemon says where each start's time went, shows that the growth is the replay of the log and nothing else: <https://github.com/skyzfs/skyzfs/actions/runs/43023027664>, 26.04. Over 22 take-overs the log grew from 0.4 GiB to 4.3 GiB and its replay from 2.6 s to 34 s, all but about 50 ms of each start; the lock, the stored position, the wait for the device and the begin and end of the recovery took under a millisecond together. That is 140 to 160 MiB of log a second. The volume is 8 GiB: after a few minutes of a pool's writes the log was more than half that size, and nothing stops it growing past it.
[^ckptrun]: `skyzfsd` as of skyzfs/skyzfs#17, from a run-only branch with that code, on Ubuntu 24.04 (Linux 7.0.0-1017) and 26.04 (7.0.0-1019), one run per image each. The daemon rig with all checks and a step that writes 640 MiB to a pool and reads the daemon's status during it: <https://github.com/skyzfs/skyzfs/actions/runs/43102947215>, the code of fac0ed6, which the merged code differs from in tests only. The same step alone, on the code of 01d17a0: <https://github.com/skyzfs/skyzfs/actions/runs/43100512642> (the replay of 0.3 to 0.4 s). An earlier whole rig, on the code of e57dd4b: <https://github.com/skyzfs/skyzfs/actions/runs/43094385803>. The figures are from the daemon's own start lines and status, read by the lead; the readings are on the pull request.
[^zerorun]: `skyzfsd` as of skyzfs/skyzfs#19, from a run-only branch with that code, one run per image: <https://github.com/skyzfs/skyzfs/actions/runs/43117301373>. The device is zeroed before the pool is made: once over 128 MiB, and in a second arm eight times over its 4 GiB, which fills the log to the high mark with 32,768 write-zeroes records. In that arm the count of steps rose from 6 to 4,104 while the first file was written. The status is read three times in the first 0.9 s of each file and then every 10 s, so the times of the later files are upper bounds. The code before the change was not run on a kernel in this state; its growth is shown by the unit tests added with the change, which fail on it. Read by the lead; the reading is on the pull request.
[^zerohole]: `skyzfsd` as of skyzfs/skyzfs#21 at 058174f, from a run-only branch with that code, one run per image: <https://github.com/skyzfs/skyzfs/actions/runs/43127606884>. Each arm zeroes the raw device eight times over its 4 GiB before the pool is made, writes five files of 128 MiB with `fsync`, scrubs, stops, starts again, scrubs and compares the files. The second arm serves with the test build's `--refuse-holes`, which makes the store behave as on a file system that cannot deallocate; no file system that itself refuses was run. The times are the script's bounds, as in the note above. The rule on the file system's block size and the end of a step at a refusal were added in review, after this run: they rest on unit tests and on the pull request's rig runs, which zero no device. Read by the lead; the reading is on the pull request.
[^zfsnozero]: OpenZFS 2.4.1 has no use of `REQ_OP_WRITE_ZEROES` or `blkdev_issue_zeroout` under `module/` or `include/`. Its disk vdev trims with `__blkdev_issue_discard` and `blkdev_issue_secure_erase` (`module/os/linux/zfs/vdev_disk.c`).
[^readerr]: `module/zfs/zio.c`, `zio_done`: a failed read is marked for suspension only when its error is `ENXIO`. Any other error on a zio issued with `ZIO_FLAG_CANFAIL`, which demand reads are (`module/zfs/dbuf.c`), is returned to the caller. `zio_vdev_io_assess` turns a leaf error into `ENXIO` only once the vdev is no longer accessible. Slow I/O is reported after `zfs_deadman_ziotime_ms` (`module/zfs/spa_misc.c`), and with the default `zfs_deadman_failmode=wait` it is only reported. Read in upstream master on 2026-11-10.
[^roundde]: `topology-bench.yml` by dispatch from the testing branch at 1b8887d, daemon de83464: runs 43265803362 (Ubuntu 24.04) and 43265811438 (Ubuntu 26.04), 120 s per row. The M1.3n round's 24.04 job is run 43250509791.
